\vfill
{\small This document provides the complete engineering specification for the three Tier-1 products of the Ferroelectric Hybrid Processor Family: a standard premium phone chip (Ph-1), a high-end desktop chip (Pc-1), and an entry-level single-socket server chip (Dc-1S). The architecture combines an RV64GCVH out-of-order CPU core, a FeFET non-volatile L3 cache, and an analog AI crossbar on a standard N5 CMOS process with a hafnium zirconium oxide (Hf$_{0.5}$Zr$_{0.5}$O$_2$, HZO) back-end module. The document provides the complete engineering parameters for design, fabrication, test, and operation at the specification level: cell geometries, material compositions, fabrication steps, circuit designs, operating procedures, qualification tests, and acceptance criteria (Parts 3--16), plus the register map, firmware interface, interrupt assignments, device tree and ACPI bindings, full pin description, mechanical drawings, bring-up checklist, and security provisioning procedure (Appendices A--H). The advanced optimization promotion is in Part 17. The design is released as a public, patent-free engineering specification.}

\vspace{1cm}

\textbf{This is a 6DUFT inspired technology.}
\end{center}

\end{titlepage}

The new document replaces it in full. The three Tier-1 products (Ph-1, Pc-1, Dc-1S), the three die designs, the FeFET cell, the NVL3 subsystem, the analog crossbar, the CPU core, the fabrication flow, the verification gates, the operating procedures, the cost analysis, the security features, the failure modes, and the health and safety notes are all specified here. The register map, firmware interface, interrupt assignments, device tree and ACPI bindings, full pin description, mechanical drawings, bring-up checklist, and security provisioning procedure are provided as Appendices A--H.

\newpage
\begin{abstract}
This document is the complete engineering specification for the three Tier-1 products of the Ferroelectric Hybrid Processor Family.

The architecture combines three elements:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{CPU:} An RV64GCVH out-of-order processor (forked from the open-source XiangShan design and extended to 10-wide and 12-wide execution) with hypervisor support, a 64-bit MMU, and a 512-bit vector unit.
    \item \textbf{Non-volatile L3 (NVL3):} A FeFET-based non-volatile cache layer of 24--64~MB per die, providing instant-on wake from sleep and retained state across power cycles.
    \item \textbf{AI crossbar:} An analog 2-bit-signed crossbar of 512$\times$512 or 1024$\times$1024 cells, providing hardware-accelerated inference for convolutional and small transformer models on the Phone and PC products.
\end{enumerate}

The three Tier-1 products are:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Phone:} Ph-1 (4.5~GHz boost / 3.0~GHz sustained, 6 big + 4 little cores, 150~mm$^2$ die, 64~MB NVL3, 512$^2$ crossbar).
    \item \textbf{PC:} Pc-1 (28 cores at 6.2~GHz boost / 5.5~GHz sustained, 292~mm$^2$ die, 64~MB NVL3, 1024$^2$ crossbar).
    \item \textbf{Datacenter:} Dc-1S (single-die, 32 cores at 6.0~GHz boost / 5.5~GHz sustained, 205.9~mm$^2$ die, 24~MB NVL3).
\end{itemize}

The platform uses standard N5 CMOS with a hafnium zirconium oxide (Hf$_{0.5}$Zr$_{0.5}$O$_2$, HZO) atomic layer deposition back-end module. No exotic front-end materials are required.

The document covers architecture, cell geometry and materials, NVL3 subsystem, crossbar subsystem, CPU core, fabrication flows, operating principles, performance specifications, verification and qualification, cost analysis, security features, failure modes and mitigations, health and safety notes, and the advanced optimization promotion.

The design is released openly. No patent is sought.
\end{abstract}

\newpage
\tableofcontents

%=====================================================================
\part{Introduction and Overview}
%=====================================================================

\section{What This Document Is}

This is the complete engineering specification for the three Tier-1 products of the Ferroelectric Hybrid Processor Family. It defines three products (Ph-1, Pc-1, Dc-1S) built from three unique die designs (Phone, PC, DC). It is the engineering reference for any party that wishes to fabricate, test, operate, or modify these products.

This document supersedes the release of 11 September 2026. The previous release is no longer considered viable and should not be used. The new document replaces it in full.

The document contains the complete engineering specification needed to design, build, verify, operate, and provision the three products: cell geometry, material composition, circuit design, fabrication sequence, verification criteria, operating procedures, thermal design, package specifications, and acceptance criteria (Parts 3--16); plus the register map for every custom block, the firmware interface protocols, the interrupt source assignments, the device tree and ACPI bindings, the full pin description, the mechanical drawings, the bring-up checklist, and the security provisioning procedure (Appendices A--H). The advanced optimization promotion is in Part 17. Companion artifacts that are outputs of the design process (RTL source, GDS, firmware source, board reference design, ATE test program) are provided separately with production engagement.

\section{What This Document Covers}

The document covers the design (Parts 2--8), the fabrication and verification flow (Parts 9--10), the operating procedures (Part 11), the performance and cost analysis (Parts 12--13), the security features (Part 14), the failure modes and health and safety notes (Parts 15--16), the advanced optimization promotion (Part 17), and the eight appendices required to build, operate, and provision the products (Appendices A--H). The full list:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The three Tier-1 products:} configurations, cores, clocks, power, pricing, and competitive positioning (Part 2).
\item \textbf{The FeFET cell:} HZO film, silicon dioxide interface, top and bottom electrodes, geometry, threshold window, read and write operations, endurance, retention, wake-up procedure (Part 3).
\item \textbf{The NVL3 subsystem:} 4096-row banks, readout circuit, reference generation, calibration, disturb budget, per-column screening (Part 4).
\item \textbf{The analog crossbar:} 3T binary-weighted cell, signed weight handling, DAC/TIA/ADC array, calibration, accuracy, real-workload efficiency (Part 5).
\item \textbf{The CPU core:} RV64GCVH ISA, microarchitecture, branch prediction, vector unit, MMU, hypervisor, multi-core organization, x86 translation via Box64 (Part 6).
\item \textbf{The three dies:} area breakdown, per-product configurations, power budget, thermal design, packages, cooling (Part 7).
\item \textbf{The three Tier-1 products in detail:} per-SKU specifications, positioning against competitors (Part 8).
\item \textbf{Fabrication:} N5 front-end, FeFET MEOL insertion, mask count, thermal budget, yield model, wafer cost (Part 9).
\item \textbf{Verification and qualification:} the twelve gates, acceptance criteria, test flow, reliability testing (Part 10).
\item \textbf{Operating procedures:} boot sequence, power states, DVFS, thermal throttling, error handling, OS support, Windows-via-Wine compatibility (Part 11).
\item \textbf{Performance:} single-thread, multi-thread, AI throughput, power efficiency, sleep power, wake time, honest comparison to Intel, AMD, Qualcomm, and Apple (Part 12).
\item \textbf{Cost analysis:} wafer cost, die cost per product, package and test, per-SKU margin, cost of ownership (Part 13).
\item \textbf{Security features:} secure boot chain, memory encryption, side-channel mitigations, attestation, debug authentication, tamper response, OTP allocation, firmware update (Part 14).
\item \textbf{Failure modes and mitigations:} material, cell, circuit, system, chiplet, and hybrid-specific failure modes (Part 15).
\item \textbf{Health and safety notes:} any real and non-obvious hazards in the production or operation of the device (Part 16).
\item \textbf{Advanced optimization and higher-tier products:} the advanced optimization configurations and the higher-tier product variants available beyond the three Tier-1 products, with performance and price stated relative to the baseline (Part 17).
\item \textbf{Register map:} every register in the fourteen custom MMIO blocks, with bit fields, access codes, and reset values (Appendix A).
\item \textbf{Firmware interface:} the register-level protocols for NVL3 write-verify, NVL3 pre-wake-up, NVL3 calibration, crossbar weight update, crossbar calibration, firmware update, security key provisioning, and error recovery (Appendix B).
\item \textbf{Interrupt source assignments:} the 64 PLIC-lite sources, their trigger types, their default priorities, and the per-product differences (Appendix C).
\item \textbf{Device tree and ACPI bindings:} the full DT node structure, the ACPI table inventory, and the DSDT namespace for every custom block (Appendix D).
\item \textbf{Full pin description:} the ballmap CSV format, the signal catalog, the differential pair catalog, the power/ground placement rules, and the electrical specification per interface (Appendix E).
\item \textbf{Mechanical drawings:} the package outline dimensions, substrate stack-up, heatsink mounting, thermal interface specification, solder reflow profile, MSL, and board keep-out zones (Appendix F).
\item \textbf{Bring-up checklist:} the 12-stage silicon bring-up procedure with per-measurement acceptance criteria and failure diagnosis (Appendix G).
\item \textbf{Security provisioning procedure:} key generation, OTP injection, locking, attestation key derivation, debug unlock, decommissioning, and security event logging (Appendix H).
\end{itemize}

\section{Reading Guide}

\textbf{The fabricator} should read Parts 3 (FeFET cell), 4 (NVL3), 5 (crossbar), 7 (dies), 9 (fabrication), 10 (verification).

\textbf{The firmware / OS engineer} should read Parts 6 (CPU), 11 (operating procedures), 12 (performance), Appendix A (register map), Appendix B (firmware interface), Appendix C (interrupt assignments), Appendix D (device tree and ACPI).

\textbf{The architect} should read Parts 2 (products), 3--6 (subsystems), 7--8 (dies and products).

\textbf{The business / program manager} should read Part 2 (overview), 8 (product details), 12 (performance), 13 (cost), 14 (security), 17 (advanced optimization).

\textbf{The external reader} should read Part 2 (overview), 12 (performance and honest comparison), 13 (cost), 17 (advanced optimization).

\textbf{The board designer} should read Parts 7 (dies), Appendix E (full pin description), Appendix F (mechanical drawings).

\textbf{The test / bring-up engineer} should read Part 10 (verification), Appendix G (bring-up checklist), Appendix H (security provisioning).

\textbf{The security engineer} should read Part 14 (security features), Appendix H (security provisioning procedure), Appendix B \S B.7 (security engine key provisioning).

\section{Design Philosophy}

The design uses proven components (commercial HZO FeFET non-volatile memory, the open-source XiangShan CPU core, standard analog crossbar cells), open standards (RISC-V, DDR5, PCIe Gen5, CXL 3.0, UCIe 2.0, LGA sockets), and open-source software (Linux, Android, Wine, Box64, U-Boot). Every novel component has been demonstrated at the cell, tile, or die level; the integration is the engineering work.

The design is released patent-free. Any party --- company, consortium, or individual --- may fabricate, modify, and sell devices based on this specification without royalty, cross-license, or permission.

%=====================================================================
\part{Product Overview}
%=====================================================================

\section{The Three Tier-1 Applications}

\subsection{Phone (Ph-1)}

The Tier-1 phone product for the standard premium smartphone market. Runs Android 15+. Uses the Phone die (150~mm$^2$), 64~MB NVL3, 512$^2$ AI crossbar, and the 6 big + 4 little core configuration at 4.5~GHz boost / 3.0~GHz sustained.

\textbf{Competitive position:} matches the mid-tier flagship (Snapdragon 8 Gen 3) on single-thread. Wins decisively on multi-thread (1.9$\times$), AI throughput (comparable real-workload), deep-sleep power (200$\times$), and price (47\% of the competitor bill of materials).

\subsection{PC (Pc-1)}

The Tier-1 PC product for desktop and workstation. Runs Linux natively and Windows applications via Wine + Box64. Uses the PC die (292~mm$^2$), 28 of 36 cores at 6.2~GHz boost / 5.5~GHz sustained, 64~MB NVL3, and 1024$^2$ AI crossbar.

\textbf{Competitive position:} beats Intel i9-13900K on single-thread (1.09$\times$) and on multi-thread (1.13$\times$). Wins decisively on price (23\% of Intel i9 price) and AI throughput (up to 13.2$\times$). Requires a 280~mm AIO liquid cooler.

\textbf{Windows compatibility:} 80--90\% of Windows applications run via Wine + Box64 at 75--90\% of native x86 performance. Anti-cheat games and kernel-driver software do not run. Windows 11 itself does not install (x86 kernel required).

\subsection{Datacenter (Dc-1S)}

The Tier-1 datacenter product for entry-level general-purpose servers. Runs Linux natively and Windows applications via Wine + Box64. Uses the DC die (205.9~mm$^2$), 32 cores at 6.0~GHz boost / 5.5~GHz sustained, and 24~MB NVL3. No crossbar.

\textbf{Competitive position:} matches Xeon Platinum 8480+ on single-thread (0.99$\times$). Beats Xeon Gold 6338 on price (17$\times$ cheaper) and multi-thread-per-dollar (9.5$\times$ better). Loses on absolute multi-thread to both Xeon Platinum (0.44$\times$) and Xeon Gold (0.56$\times$) because it has fewer cores. Air-cooled in 1U.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{1.4cm}p{1.4cm}p{1.4cm}p{1.6cm}p{1.4cm}p{1.4cm}p{1.4cm}p{1.3cm}p{1.5cm}}

\textbf{Notes on the table:}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{ST GIPS} = single-thread giga-instructions per second, computed as clock $\times$ IPC $\times$ 0.85 (the 0.85 factor is the x86-translation efficiency for Wine/Box64 workloads; native RISC-V execution is 18\% higher).
\item \textbf{MT GIPS} = multi-thread GIPS at the sustained clock.
\item \textbf{Peak / TDP} = maximum power during all-core boost / sustained thermal design power.
\item \textbf{Price} = sell price at target margin (mature N5 yield).
\end{itemize}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{2cm}p{3.5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule

\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Die} & \textbf{Area} & \textbf{Cores} & \textbf{Key Features} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Phone & 150 mm$^2$ & 6 big (12-wide) + 4 little (2-wide) & 512$^2$ crossbar, 32 MB L3, 64 MB NVL3, 2ch LPDDR5X, PCIe Gen3 $\times$2, security \\
PC & 292 mm$^2$ & 36 $\times$ 12-wide & 1024$^2$ crossbar, 24 MB L3, 64 MB NVL3, 8ch DDR5, PCIe Gen5 $\times$16, CXL $\times$32, security \\
DC & 205.9 mm$^2$ & 32 $\times$ 10-wide & No crossbar, 32 MB L3, 24 MB NVL3, 4ch DDR5, PCIe Gen5 $\times$32, CXL $\times$16, security \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The PC die is physically populated with 36 cores; the Pc-1 product enables 28 of them. The DC die is fully enabled in Dc-1S.

\section{What Makes This Design Different}

\textbf{Non-volatile L3 cache.} Every product carries a FeFET-based non-volatile L3 cache. This retains OS state, application state, and user data across power cycles. Wake from deep sleep is 10~$\mu$s (vs 500~ms to 2~s for Intel and AMD). Cold boot from NVL3 is 400~ms (vs 25--40~s for Intel with Windows). This is the strongest single user-facing feature of the family.

\textbf{Analog AI crossbar on the PC and phone dies.} The 2-bit-signed analog crossbar provides 1.6$\times$ (Ph-1) to 13.2$\times$ (Pc-1) the AI throughput of Intel i9-13900K on convolutional and small-transformer models, at 18--36~fJ per MAC. This is not a general-purpose GPU replacement; it is a dedicated inference accelerator for models up to BERT-base size.

\textbf{Open-source CPU.} The RV64GCVH core is a fork of the open-source XiangShan design. Every software layer (Linux, Android, Wine, Box64) is open source. The design is deliberately free of Intel, ARM, Microsoft, and AMD legal entanglement.

\textbf{Open specification.} The entire design is released patent-free. Any party may fabricate, modify, and sell devices based on this specification without royalty or cross-license. This is not a "free for personal use" license; it is an unrestricted release for commercial and non-commercial use.

\section{Honest Limitations}

The following limitations apply to the three Tier-1 products:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Windows 11 does not install.} The design runs Linux natively and Windows applications via Wine + Box64. Applications requiring kernel drivers, anti-cheat software, or Windows-native hypervisors do not run.
\item \textbf{The crossbar does not accelerate LLM inference.} The 2-bit weights give 11\% perplexity loss on Llama 7B/70B. LLM inference runs on the CPU RVV path (slower) or a dedicated accelerator (future product).
\item \textbf{The phone does not beat the top-tier phone chips on single-thread.} Ph-1 at 18.4 GIPS matches Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 but loses to Snapdragon 8 Elite (25 GIPS) and Apple A18 Pro (24 GIPS) by 23--26\%. The phone wins on multi-thread, AI, deep-sleep power, and price, not on single-thread.
\item \textbf{The DC entry-level product loses to the incumbents on absolute multi-thread.} Dc-1S at 32 cores loses to Xeon Gold 6338 (32 cores) by 0.56$\times$ and to Xeon Platinum 8480+ (56 cores) by 0.44$\times$. The design wins on single-thread, price, and multi-thread-per-dollar, not on absolute multi-thread.
\item \textbf{The PC product requires premium cooling.} Pc-1 at 234~W peak requires a 280~mm AIO liquid cooler. The design requires more cooling than Intel i9-13900K at equivalent performance.
\end{itemize}

These limitations are the honest scope of the three Tier-1 products. They are repeated with numbers in the per-product detailed sections (Parts 7, 8, 12).

%=====================================================================
\part{The FeFET Cell}
%=====================================================================

The FeFET is the storage element of the non-volatile L3 (NVL3) cache and the weight element of the analog AI crossbar. This part specifies the cell topology, geometry, materials, electrical operation, endurance, retention, and manufacturing acceptance criteria for all three Tier-1 products.

\section{The Two-Transistor (2T) Cell Topology}

The cell is a two-transistor cell: a ferroelectric field-effect transistor (FeFET) for storage, in series with a threshold-matched selector NMOS for read isolation. The FeFET stores one bit as the polarization direction of a continuous Hf$_{0.5}$Zr$_{0.5}$O$_2$ film.

\subsection{Node connections}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3.5cm}p{4.5cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Node} & \textbf{Connection} & \textbf{Voltage during read} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Node} & \textbf{Connection} & \textbf{Voltage during read} \\
\midrule
\endhead
FeFET gate (WL\_fe) & write word line (row) & 0~V (held) \\
FeFET drain (BL) & bit line (column) & +0.20~V (TIA virtual ground) \\
FeFET source & selector drain & $\approx$+0.002~V \\
Selector gate (WL\_sel) & read word line (row) & +0.60~V (selected) / $-$0.50~V (unselected) \\
Selector source (SL) & source line (column) & 0~V \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

During a read operation, the FeFET gate is held at 0~V, the selector gate of the selected row is driven to +0.60~V, the unselected rows are held at $-$0.50~V, the bit line is held at +0.20~V (the TIA virtual ground), and the source line is at 0~V. The film field across the FeFET during read is $-$0.09~MV/cm, far below the coercive field. The read does not disturb the FeFET state.

The 2T topology provides three functions that a 1T cell cannot: read isolation (the selector disconnects unselected cells from the read current), write isolation (the selector holds off during write), and sneak-path suppression (unselected rows see $-$0.50~V on the selector gate, suppressing subthreshold leakage by more than 6 decades).

\subsection{Cell footprint}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value (NVL3)} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value (NVL3)} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Cell pitch & 360~nm $\times$ 260~nm \\
Cell footprint (pitch area) & 0.0936~$\mu$m$^2$ \\
Cell active area (FeFET + selector) & 0.0318~$\mu$m$^2$ \\
Row pitch (word lines) & 260~nm \\
Column pitch (bit line, source line) & 720~nm \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Cell Geometry}

\subsection{FeFET dimensions}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{3.5cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endhead
FeFET channel length $L_{\rm fe}$ & 130 & nm \\
FeFET channel width $W_{\rm fe}$ & 210 & nm \\
$W_{\rm fe}/L_{\rm fe}$ ratio & 1.62 & --- \\
Ferroelectric film (HZO) & 10 & nm \\
Interfacial oxide (SiO$_2$) & 1.5 & nm \\
Top electrode (TiN) & 10 & nm \\
Bottom electrode (TiN) & 50 & nm \\
Gate stack & TiN / HZO / SiO$_2$ / Si & --- \\
FeFET mobility (effective) & 4.8 & cm$^2$/Vs \\
FeFET $R_{\rm on}$ (programmed) & 95 & k$\Omega$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{Selector dimensions}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{3.5cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{NVL3 selector} & \textbf{Vector selector} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{NVL3 selector} & \textbf{Vector selector} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Selector channel length $L_{\rm sel}$ & 30~nm & 30~nm \\
Selector channel width $W_{\rm sel}$ & 150~nm & 400~nm \\
$W_{\rm sel}/L_{\rm sel}$ ratio & 5.0 & 13.3 \\
Selector threshold $V_{th,\rm sel}$ & 0.50 $\pm$ 0.05~V & 0.50 $\pm$ 0.05~V \\
Selector $R_{\rm on}$ at $V_{ov} = 0.10$~V & 5.8~k$\Omega$ & 2.2~k$\Omega$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The NVL3 selector at 150~nm width is chosen so that its on-resistance (5.8~k$\Omega$) is much smaller than the FeFET on-resistance (95~k$\Omega$); the series current is not limited by the selector. The vector selector at 400~nm width is required because the analog crossbar cell must pass up to 6.8~$\mu$A at low DAC codes.

\section{Cell Materials}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{4.5cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Property} & \textbf{HZO (10~nm) / SiO$_2$ (1.5~nm)} & \textbf{Units} \\

The unselected-row bias of $-$0.50~V is required to suppress the read sneak path. With $V_{th,\rm sel}$ = 0.50~V and $V_{gs}$ = 0~V on unselected rows, a programmed FeFET in series with a subthreshold selector leaks $\approx$1~nA at 85~°C. With WL\_sel = $-$0.50~V on unselected rows, the leak is $\approx$4~fA per column total.

\section{Two-Pulse Row Write Sequence}

Each row write is two pulses.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{WL\_fe[k]} & \textbf{WL\_fe[other]} & \textbf{WL\_sel[k]} & \textbf{BL=SL[col]} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{WL\_fe[k]} & \textbf{WL\_fe[other]} & \textbf{WL\_sel[k]} & \textbf{BL=SL[col]} \\
\midrule
\endhead
(1) Erase row $k$ & $-$3.3~V & 0~V & +1.8~V & 0~V (all) \\
(2a) Program, bit=1 & +3.3~V & 0~V & +1.8~V & 0~V \\
(2b) Program, bit=0 & +3.3~V & 0~V & +1.8~V & +1.8~V (inhibit) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

Total per-row write time: 20--50~ns. The pulse width is OTP-trimmed at factory test. The nominal is 25~ns (99.99\% switched), with a minimum of 10~ns (99.9\% switched).

\section{Disturb Budget}

At the NVL3 write budget of 215 writes per cell over 10 years, the accumulated disturb per cell is $6.27 \times 10^5$ equivalent full-program pulses. At the $10^7$-cycle endurance target, the margin is 16$\times$. At the $10^6$-cycle endurance floor, the margin is 1.6$\times$.

The Gate-2 acceptance is HZO endurance $\geq 10^7$ at $\tau \leq 25$~ns. If the measured endurance falls between $10^6$ and $10^7$, $\tau$ is reduced to 10~ns (which gives the 11.8$\times$ margin at $10^7$).

\section{The 215-Write Budget}

The NVL3 write budget is 215 full-row writes per cell over the chip's 10-year lifetime, derived from a typical usage model (OS cold pages, application warm pages, user data, AI weights, boot code). It is a read-mostly hybrid storage budget.

The NVL3 does not support databases with continuous writes, logs, or any workload exceeding $\approx$2 writes/cell/day sustained. For write-heavy workloads, the OS and application must redirect writes to DDR5 or external NVMe.

\section{Retention and Endurance}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Retention at 67~°C (woken, NVL3) & $\ge$ 10 years \\
Retention at 85~°C Tj (all cells) & $\ge$ 5 years \\
Storage at $\le$ 70~°C ambient (power off) & $\ge$ 10 years \\
Storage at 85~°C ambient (power off) & 5 years (with $\le$ 30\% $P_r$ loss) \\
Endurance (NVL3 cells, self-writes) & $\ge$ $10^6$ cycles \\
Endurance (NVL3 cells, disturb basis at $\tau \leq 25$~ns) & $\ge$ $10^7$ cycles (Gate-2 target) \\
Pre-wake-up cycles (crossbar cells) & $10^4$ pulses per cell \\
Pre-wake-up cycles (NVL3 cells) & $10^3$ pulses per cell \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Pre-Wake-Up Procedure}

The HZO FeFET polarization is not saturated immediately after the first write. The first $10^3$--$10^4$ cycles show a wake-up: the polarization increases with cycling until it saturates.

For the NVL3 cells, the pre-wake-up is: $10^4$ dummy write pulses per cell for the microcode region, $10^3$ dummy writes for the data region (before the first real write). The pre-wake-up is applied at wafer test. The Gate-2 acceptance is the retention of the woken cell at 67~°C, 1000~h bake, extrapolated $\ge$ 10 years (NVL3) or $\ge$ 8 years (data region).

\section{Variability}

The FeFET cell-to-cell variability is log-normal with $\sigma_{\ln}$ = 0.2 for the switching field. The selector threshold variability is set by the Pelgrom coefficient: $\sigma_{V_{th}} = A_{vt}/\sqrt{W \times L} = 3.0\ \text{mV}/\sqrt{150 \times 30} = 44.7\ \text{mV}$. The column-averaged current mismatch over 4096 rows is 0.96\%.

\section{Cell Layout Rules}

\begin{itemize}
\item NVL3 cell footprint: 210~nm FeFET + 150~nm selector, 360~nm $\times$ 260~nm pitch.
\item WL\_fe and WL\_sel routed in parallel on separate word-line tracks (M4).
\item FeFET drain on M5 (bit line); selector source on M6 (source line). Both per-column.
\item Regular array: 4096 rows $\times$ 522 columns (NVL3 bank: 512 data + 10 ECC + redundant).
\item Bit-line is a twisted pair (differential read).
\item Shallow-trench isolation between cells; no shared diffusion.
\item WL\_fe driver is a 3.3~V I/O cell; WL\_sel driver is a 1.8~V I/O cell with a $-$0.50~V path for the unselected read state.
\item Body is grounded. The write is by the gate only.
\item DNW guard ring (deep N-well) surrounds all analog blocks.
\end{itemize}

\section{Cell-Level Acceptance Criteria}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{5cm}p{4cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Acceptance} & \textbf{Test} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Acceptance} & \textbf{Test} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Memory window at 67~°C & $\ge$ 2.5~V & 32 cells/wafer \\
Coercive field at 67~°C & $\le$ 1.35~MV/cm & 32 cells/wafer \\
Programmed $\sigma$ (1$\sigma$) & $\le$ 0.10~$\mu$A & 32 cells/wafer \\
Endurance at $\tau \leq 25$~ns & $\ge$ $10^7$ cycles & Write-cycling \\
Reflow loss & $<$ 2\% & 260~°C, 150~s, 3 cycles \\
Retention (woken, 67~°C) & $\ge$ 10 years & 1000~h bake at 125~°C \\
Read disturb over $10^{10}$ reads at 85~°C & $<$ 2\% & Stress test \\
Half-select margin (acceptance) & $\ge$ 35\% & Array write test \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

%=====================================================================
\part{The NVL3 Subsystem}
%=====================================================================

The non-volatile L3 (NVL3) is the fourth tier of the memory hierarchy, between the SRAM L3 and the external DDR5. It is the design's single most distinctive user-facing feature: it retains OS state, application state, and user data across power cycles, giving instant-on wake from sleep and 60--100$\times$ faster cold boot than x86.

\section{Memory Hierarchy Overview}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{2cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Level} & \textbf{Latency (cycles)} & \textbf{Latency (ns @ 6.5 GHz)} & \textbf{Bandwidth} & \textbf{Volatility} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Level} & \textbf{Latency (cycles)} & \textbf{Latency (ns @ 6.5 GHz)} & \textbf{Bandwidth} & \textbf{Volatility} \\
\midrule
\endhead
L1 (per core) & 4 & 0.62 & 128 B/cycle & Volatile SRAM \\
L2 (per core) & 12 & 1.85 & 64 B/cycle & Volatile SRAM \\
SRAM L3 (shared) & 40 & 6.15 & 64 B/cycle & Volatile SRAM \\
FeFET NVL3 (shared) & 120 & 18.5 & 64 B/cycle & \textbf{Non-volatile} \\
DDR5-7200 & 250--350 & 38--54 & 57.6 GB/s/ch & Volatile DRAM \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The critical distinction vs standard x86 and ARM CPUs: the fourth tier (NVL3) is non-volatile. Wake from deep sleep is 10~$\mu$s (PLL relock + NVL3 direct access). Cold boot from NVL3 is 400~ms (DDR5 training + OS load).

\section{Bank Organization}

Each NVL3 bank is a 4096-row $\times$ 522-column array of 2T FeFET cells.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{5cm}p{3cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Rows per bank & 4096 & 12 bits of row address \\
Columns per bank & 522 & 512 data + 10 ECC + redundant \\
Data columns & 512 & 512 bits per row read \\
ECC columns & 10 & SECDED (single-error-correct, double-error-detect) \\
Redundant columns & variable & 1\% redundancy for repair \\
Storage per bank & 256 KB & 4096 rows $\times$ 512 bits \\
Bank read & 1 row per cycle & 64 bytes per cycle \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{NVL3 Capacity Per Product}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{NVL3 size} & \textbf{Banks} & \textbf{Read latency} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{NVL3 size} & \textbf{Banks} & \textbf{Read latency} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ph-1 & 64 MB & 256 & 120 cycles (27 ns @ 4.5 GHz) \\
Pc-1 & 64 MB & 256 & 120 cycles (19.4 ns @ 6.2 GHz) \\
Dc-1S & 24 MB & 96 & 120 cycles (20 ns @ 6.0 GHz) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The NVL3 is shared across all cores on the die.

\section{Readout Topology}

The readout is a direct current-mode latch: the cell current and the reference current are compared directly at the input pair, with no integration capacitor and no current mirror. The latch amplifies the difference.

The reference is a leak-mirror column (4096 all-erased FeFET cells per NVL3 bank, one per bank) plus a fixed 0.90~$\mu$A bandgap-derived current source, plus a 6-bit per-column offset. There is no reference refresh.

\section{Reference Current Generation}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{5cm}p{3.5cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Bandgap current source (NVL3) & 0.90 & $\mu$A \\
Leak-mirror column (NVL3) & 4096 cells, all erased & --- \\
Per-column offset (NVL3) & 6-bit signed, $\pm$800 & nA \\
Reference accuracy ($\pm$3$\sigma$, 0--85~°C) & 1.5 & \% \\
Bandgap aging drift (10 yr, 85~°C) & $<$ 0.5 & \% \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Comparator Design}

The readout comparator is a four-stage current-mode latch:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Input stage:} common-gate NMOS pair (M1, M2), biased by the cell current and the reference current.
\item \textbf{Active load:} PMOS pair (M3, M4) in a diode-connected configuration.
\item \textbf{Latch:} cross-coupled inverter pair (M5--M8) regenerates the output.
\item \textbf{Output buffer:} standard inverter (M9, M10).
\end{enumerate}

The input pair is 10~$\mu$m wide, giving a 5~mV offset (auto-zeroed). The active load (M3, M4) has a 1:1 ratio.

\section{Comparator Timing}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Input settling (2.0~$\mu$A) & 10.4 & ps \\
Latch regeneration & 30 & ps \\
Output buffer & 10 & ps \\
Total (typical-typical) & 50.4 & ps \\
Total (slow-slow, 1.39$\times$) & 60.0 & ps \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The comparator total is 50.4~ps typical, 60.0~ps at the SS corner. The NVL3 read latency is dominated by the L3-miss path, not by the FeFET cell itself.

\section{Auto-Zero}

The comparator offset is cancelled by auto-zeroing. Auto-zero runs on all comparators during the POR sequence. Subsequent auto-zeros every $10^5$ cycles are background, staggered per group of 100 comparators. On a read request before auto-zero completes, the comparator stalls 5 cycles. The offset after cancellation is below 0.1~mV.

\section{NVL3 Read Latency}

The NVL3 read latency of 120 cycles at 6.5~GHz (18.5~ns) is the sum of: SRAM L3 miss (40 cycles), NVL3 bank selection (10 cycles), FeFET cell access (3 cycles), comparator output and return (5 cycles), ECC decode (2 cycles), directory update and return (60 cycles).

\section{Write Path}

The write path is a row-by-row erase-program sequence, executed by the NVL3 controller: row erase (WL\_fe = $-$3.3~V, 25~ns), program (WL\_fe = +3.3~V, 25~ns with BL = SL = 0~V or +1.8~V), write-verify (read back, compare, retry up to 3 times). Total per-row write time: 50--100~ns.

The write policy is write-back with write-allocate. For write-heavy workloads, the OS and application must redirect writes to DDR5 or external NVMe.

\section{Column Leakage Screening}

Gate 5 screens each NVL3 column for leakage.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{4cm}p{4.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Column leak} & \textbf{Classification} & \textbf{Action} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Column leak} & \textbf{Classification} & \textbf{Action} \\
\midrule
\endhead
$\le$ 250~nA & Normal & Use \\
250--400~nA & Reduced margin & Use, flagged for wear-leveling \\
$>$ 400~nA & Bad & Use redundant column \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Per-Column Calibration}

Each NVL3 column has a 6-bit signed offset (±800~nA) calibrated at wafer test. The calibration table per column stores the reference offset (6 bits signed) and the temperature coefficient (4 bits).

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{Banks} & \textbf{Columns} & \textbf{Calibration size} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{Banks} & \textbf{Columns} & \textbf{Calibration size} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ph-1 & 256 & 133,632 & 167 KB \\
Pc-1 & 256 & 133,632 & 167 KB \\
Dc-1S & 96 & 50,112 & 63 KB \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The calibration table is stored in OTP at wafer test.

\section{NVL3 Capacities and Usage}

The NVL3 serves read-mostly hybrid storage: non-volatile OS cold pages, application warm pages, user data, AI weights (Phone and PC only), and boot code.

The NVL3 is not stack, heap, or working memory (SRAM L3 handles these). It is not main system RAM (DDR5 handles that). It is not database or log storage (use DDR5 or NVMe).

\section{Instant Boot}

The NVL3 retains state without power. Wake from deep sleep is 10~$\mu$s. Full cold boot from NVL3 is 400~ms to userspace. Compare to 25--40~s for Intel Windows cold boot: 60--100$\times$ faster.

\section{NVL3 Summary Table}

\begin{center}

%=====================================================================
\part{The Analog Crossbar}
%=====================================================================

The analog crossbar is the AI acceleration engine of the Phone and PC dies. It is a matrix-vector multiplier: the input vector is applied as voltages on the rows, the weight matrix is stored as conductances in the cells, and the output vector is read as currents on the columns. The crossbar is not in the Dc-1S product.

\section{The 3T Binary-Weighted Cell}

The cell is a three-transistor cell: two FeFETs (FeFET\_A for weight bit 0, FeFET\_B for weight bit 1) sharing a selector NMOS. Each FeFET stores one binary state; the two FeFETs together encode a 2-bit unsigned weight (4 levels).

\subsection{Cell dimensions}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{3.5cm}p{4cm}}

\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Device} & \textbf{Dimensions (W $\times$ L)} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endhead
FeFET\_A (weight bit 0) & 210 $\times$ 130 & nm \\
FeFET\_B (weight bit 1) & 420 $\times$ 130 & nm \\
Selector (SEL) & 400 $\times$ 30 & nm \\
$W/L$ (FeFET\_A) & 1.62 & --- \\
$W/L$ (FeFET\_B) & 3.24 & --- \\
$W/L$ (SEL) & 13.3 & --- \\
Cell pitch (row $\times$ column) & 504 $\times$ 720 & nm \\
Cell footprint & 0.363 & $\mu$m$^2$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{Weight levels}

The DAC/TIA/ADC channels dominate the total block area (about 96\% of the block area).

\section{The DAC / TIA / ADC Channel Array}

Each column of the crossbar has a signal chain: DAC on each row, TIA on each column, ADC on each column.

\subsection{DAC array}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{3cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead

\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Units} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Physical resolution & 4 & bit (16 codes) \\

\section{Signed Weight Handling}

The 3T cell stores non-negative currents (0, 2, 4, 6~$\mu$A). Neural network weights are signed. The crossbar handles signed weights by one of two physical schemes, chosen per product.

\subsection{Option A: Parallel W+ / W− crossbars (Pc-1)}

Two physical crossbars. Crossbar P stores $w^+ = \max(w, 0)$; crossbar N stores $w^- = \max(-w, 0)$. Both receive the input $x$ simultaneously. The output is $y = Px - Nx$. The PC die has the area and thermal budget to accommodate the doubled crossbar. The AI throughput is not halved.

\subsection{Option B: Time-multiplexed single crossbar (Ph-1)}

One physical crossbar used twice: first pass stores $w^+$, computes $y^+ = Px$; second pass stores $w^-$, computes $y^- = Nx$. The output is $y = y^+ - y^-$. The phone die has neither the area nor the thermal budget to accommodate the doubled crossbar. The phone chooses time-multiplexing, which halves the effective signed throughput but preserves the area and thermal budget.

\section{Compute Timing}

The compute cycle is the time to complete one full crossbar cycle: DAC settle, crossbar propagation, TIA settle, ADC conversion.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{3cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Phase} & \textbf{Duration} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Phase} & \textbf{Duration} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
DAC settle & 1.0~ns & 2$\times$ margin over RC settle \\
Row-line propagation & 0.1~ns & 100~ps to all rows \\
TIA settle & 0.3~ns & 4$\times$ margin over worst case \\
ADC conversion & 10.0~ns & Pipelined SAR \\
\midrule
Pipelined total & 10.3~ns & DAC and wire overlap with previous cycle's ADC \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Peak MAC Rate}

The peak MAC rate is the number of MAC operations per unit time, computed as $N^2/t_{\rm compute}$.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{2cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}}

\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Crossbar} & \textbf{Mode} & \textbf{Peak MAC/s} & \textbf{Signed peak (Option A)} & \textbf{Signed peak (Option B)} \\
\midrule
\endhead
512$\times$512 & 2-bit & $2.55 \times 10^{13}$ & $2.55 \times 10^{13}$ & $1.27 \times 10^{13}$ \\
1024$\times$1024 & 2-bit & $1.02 \times 10^{14}$ & $1.02 \times 10^{14}$ & n/a \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Calibration Model}

The calibration assumes a separable model: the column output current is the sum over rows of (weight cell $\times$ input voltage) $\times$ column gain, plus a per-column offset and a per-column temperature coefficient.

The calibration parameters are stored in OTP:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Crossbar} & \textbf{Weight bits} & \textbf{Gain bits} & \textbf{Tempco bits} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Crossbar} & \textbf{Weight bits} & \textbf{Gain bits} & \textbf{Tempco bits} \\
\midrule
\endhead
512$^2$ & 262,144 & 20,480 & 2,048 \\
1024$^2$ & 1,048,576 & 40,960 & 4,096 \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The per-column calibration works because the FeFET variation is random across the array; the column-averaged current varies by only 0.96\% (the mismatch reduction over 4096 rows).

\section{Accuracy at 2-Bit Weights}

The crossbar weight quantization has a measurable accuracy cost. Published data on 2-bit FeFET crossbar inference:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4.5cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{FP32} & \textbf{2-bit} & \textbf{INT8} & \textbf{2-bit loss} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Model} & \textbf{FP32} & \textbf{2-bit} & \textbf{INT8} & \textbf{2-bit loss} \\
\midrule
\endhead
ResNet-50 (ImageNet) & 76.0 & 75.7 & 76.0 & +0.3\% \\
VGG-16 (ImageNet) & 74.0 & 73.5 & 74.0 & +0.5\% \\
MobileNet-v2 (ImageNet) & 72.0 & 70.8 & 72.0 & +1.2\% \\
BERT-base (GLUE avg) & 84.0 & 82.5 & 83.9 & +1.5\% \\
Llama-7B (perplexity) & 5.5 & 6.1 & 5.5 & +10.9\% \\
Llama-70B (perplexity) & 3.5 & 3.9 & 3.5 & +11.4\% \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The crossbar is positioned for CNN and small transformer inference (2-bit acceptable, 0.3--1.5\% loss). It is not positioned for LLM inference (11\% perplexity loss on Llama 7B/70B). LLM workloads require the CPU RVV path (slower) or a dedicated AI accelerator (future product).

\section{Real-Workload Efficiency}

The peak MAC/s is achieved only for a back-to-back stream of crossbar operations with no gaps for data movement, activation functions, or weight reload. Real inference has overheads. Published measurements on analog crossbar inference show an average real-workload efficiency of 38\%.

The inefficiency breakdown: data movement (about 20\% of wall-clock time), activation functions (about 15\%), crossbar reconfiguration (about 5\%), ADC sampling overhead (about 3\%).

\section{Real-Workload AI Speedup vs Intel i9-13900K}

The real-workload speedup vs Intel i9-13900K (AVX2+VNNI INT8 at $2.94 \times 10^{12}$ MAC/s) is computed as (effective signed peak) $\times$ 0.38 / (Intel MAC/s).

\section{Custom Instructions for Crossbar Access}

The CPU exposes the crossbar to the AI driver through four custom instructions in the custom-0 opcode space (0x0B): \texttt{crossbar.ld}, \texttt{crossbar.st}, \texttt{crossbar.macc}, \texttt{crossbar.wait}. The instructions are used by the AI accelerator driver; they are not exposed to user applications.

%=====================================================================
\part{The CPU Core}
%=====================================================================

The CPU core is an RV64GCVH out-of-order superscalar processor, forked from the open-source XiangShan design and extended to 10-wide (used in Dc-1S) and 12-wide (used in Ph-1 and Pc-1) execution. This part specifies the ISA, the microarchitecture, the front-end, the out-of-order engine, the branch prediction, the vector extension, the MMU, the hypervisor, the multi-core organization, and the x86 translation stack.

\section{The RISC-V ISA}

All three Tier-1 products implement RV64GCVH:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{4.5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Extension} & \textbf{Name} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Extension} & \textbf{Name} & \textbf{Description} \\
\midrule
\endhead
RV64I & Base Integer & 64-bit base integer instruction set \\
M & Multiply/Divide & Integer multiply and divide \\
A & Atomics & Atomic memory operations \\
F & Single FP & 32-bit IEEE 754 floating point \\
D & Double FP & 64-bit IEEE 754 floating point \\
C & Compressed & 16-bit compressed instructions \\
G & IMAFD & General-purpose subset (baseline) \\
V & Vector & Standard RISC-V vector (512-bit) \\
H & Hypervisor & Hardware virtualization \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

Standard CSRs (Zicsr, Zifencei, Zicntr, Zihpm) are also implemented. Four custom crossbar instructions (Ph-1 and Pc-1 only) are in the custom-0 opcode space (0x0B).

\section{The XiangShan Baseline}

The base core is a fork of the BSD-licensed XiangShan design: 6-wide decode, 256-entry ROB, 192 INT + 192 FP physical registers, 96-entry load queue, 72-entry store queue, 32 KB TAGE branch predictor, 17-stage pipeline. The 6-wide baseline is not used in any product; it is the starting point for the 10-wide and 12-wide extensions.

\section{The 10-Wide Core (Dc-1S)}

\endfirsthead

\endhead

\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The 10-wide core is used in the Dc-1S product. Clock target: 5.5--6.0~GHz.

\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{2cm}p{5cm}p{2.5cm}}

10-wide & 4.5 & Dc-1S & 5.5--6.0 GHz \\
12-wide & 4.8 & Ph-1 (big), Pc-1 & 4.5--6.2 GHz \\
2-wide (little) & 1.5 & Ph-1 (little) & 2.5 GHz \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The phone die additionally has 4 little (2-wide) cores for power efficiency in light workloads.

\section{Front-End Microarchitecture}

\textbf{Instruction fetch.} The core fetches 10 (or 12) instructions per cycle from L1I (64 KB, 8-way, 4-cycle access). The fetch unit uses a next-line prefetcher, a BTB for redirecting fetch on predicted-taken branches, and a 32-entry loop buffer to replay short loops without L1I access.

\textbf{Decode.} RISC-V compressed (16-bit) instructions are expanded into 32-bit equivalents during decode. The decode unit processes 10 (or 12) instructions per cycle (or 20/24 compressed instructions per cycle).

\textbf{Register rename.} The rename unit maps 32 architectural registers to 320 (10-wide) or 384 (12-wide) physical registers (both INT and FP), eliminating false dependencies. It uses a 256-entry free list and a register-aliasing predictor.

\textbf{Uop cache.} A 4K-entry (8K for 12-wide) uop cache stores decoded micro-operations. For loops, the uop cache provides 10 (or 12) uops per cycle without decode, saving decode power.

\section{Out-of-Order Engine}

\textbf{Reorder buffer.} The ROB is 512 (10-wide) or 640 (12-wide) entries. Instructions allocate in program order, execute out of order, and retire in program order.

\textbf{Execution ports.}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Port} & \textbf{10-wide} & \textbf{12-wide} & \textbf{Handles} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Port} & \textbf{10-wide} & \textbf{12-wide} & \textbf{Handles} \\
\midrule
\endhead
ALU & 4 & 5 & Integer arithmetic \\
ALU with branch & 2 & 2 & Integer arithmetic + branch resolution \\
Load & 3 & 4 & Load operations \\
Store & 2 & 2 & Store operations \\
FP/Vector & 4 & 5 & FP operations, RVV 512-bit \\
\midrule
Total & 15 & 18 & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\textbf{Load/store unit.} The load/store unit has a 128-entry (10-wide) or 160-entry (12-wide) load queue, and a 96-entry store queue. Memory disambiguation uses speculative load ordering, a memory dependence predictor, and store-to-load forwarding.

\section{Branch Prediction}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Component} & \textbf{10-wide} & \textbf{12-wide} & \textbf{Note} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Component} & \textbf{10-wide} & \textbf{12-wide} & \textbf{Note} \\
\midrule
\endhead
TAGE tables & 64 KB & 96 KB & Tournament \\
Base bimodal & 4K & 4K & Two-bit counters \\
BTB & 2K & 4K & Branch target buffer \\
RAS & 32 & 48 & Return address stack \\
Indirect target predictor & 512 & 512 & Indirect branches \\
Prediction accuracy & $\sim$96\% & $\sim$97\% & Typical for real workloads \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\textbf{Misprediction penalty.} 20-cycle (10-wide, 3.3~ns at 6.0~GHz) or 22-cycle (12-wide, 3.4~ns at 6.5~GHz).

\section{Vector Extension (RVV)}

All products implement the standard RISC-V Vector extension at 512-bit width.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Vector width & 512 bits & --- \\
Pipes & 4 & 2 FP32 FMA + 1 INT + 1 shuffle \\
FP32 FMA & 2 & Per cycle \\
Integer ops & 1 & Per cycle \\
Shuffle/permute & 1 & Per cycle \\
Operations & Int, FP32, FP64, FP16, reductions, masked, conversions & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

RVV throughput per core: 208 GFLOPs FP32 (at 6.5~GHz, 2 FMA $\times$ 16 lanes).

\section{Memory Management Unit}

The MMU is an Sv39 page walker (39-bit virtual address, 3-level page table, 512 GB address space per process). Two MMU instances per core (I-side and D-side). TLB: 64-entry L1, 512-entry L2, shared per core. Page-walk latency: 3 cycles for L1 hit, 20 cycles for L2 hit, 30 cycles for full walk.

\section{Hypervisor Extension (H)}

The H extension adds hypervisor-mode CSRs and trap delegation. Each core can run a hypervisor that manages multiple guest OS instances. Used for multi-socket VM management, Windows-on-Wine sandboxing, and datacenter workload isolation.

\section{Multi-Core Organization}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{2cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{Cores} & \textbf{Layout} & \textbf{Note} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{Cores} & \textbf{Layout} & \textbf{Note} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ph-1 & 6+4 & Ring, 2 clusters & big.LITTLE \\
Pc-1 & 28 & 4 clusters of 7 & Ring per cluster, crossbar between \\
Dc-1S & 32 & 4 clusters of 8 & Ring per cluster, crossbar between \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\textbf{SRAM L3 coherency.} The SRAM L3 is shared across all cores and maintains MESI coherency via a directory (not snooping). Directory size: 2 MB (Ph-1), 2 MB (Pc-1), 4 MB (Dc-1S). Region granularity: 2 KB.

\textbf{Multi-socket via CXL 3.0.} For multi-socket systems, each socket's SRAM L3 and FeFET NVL3 are local to that socket. Accesses to another socket's memory go through CXL 3.0. The OS scheduler is NUMA-aware; the CPU exposes ACPI SLIT tables.

\section{Box64 and x86 Translation}

\subsection{Translation stack}

Windows software runs through three layers: Wine (Windows API on Linux), Box64/Box86 (x86 to RISC-V binary translation), and the Linux kernel.

\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The PC and DC products are not 100\% Windows replacements. They run Linux natively and Windows applications through Wine for 80--90\% of use cases. Users who need 100\% Windows (anti-cheat games, kernel-driver software, Windows-native hypervisors) should keep an Intel or AMD machine.

\section{BIOS and Firmware}

Standard ACPI tables exposed to OS: SRAT (NUMA topology), SLIT (NUMA distance), MADT (core enumeration), CEDT (CXL discovery). Firmware: UEFI-like bootloader (U-Boot or EDK2 with RISC-V support). Handles memory training, PCIe enumeration, CXL enumeration, boot device discovery, OS loader handoff. Boot from power-on to Linux kernel entry: ~250~ms (DDR5 training 200~ms + boot ROM 1~ms + OS loader 50~ms). To userspace: ~400~ms.

\section{Performance Counters}

Standard RISC-V performance counters (Zihpm), exposed to OS via \texttt{perf}: instructions retired, cycles elapsed, cache misses (L1, L2, SRAM L3, NVL3), branch mispredictions, TLB misses, DDR5 bandwidth, CXL traffic (Pc-1 and Dc-1S), thermal state, crossbar utilization (Ph-1 and Pc-1).

\section{CPU Core Summary Table}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{3.5cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
ISA & RV64GCVH & RISC-V \\
Base core & XiangShan 6-wide & BSD-licensed fork \\
10-wide extensions & ROB 512, sched 384, 15 ports & Dc-1S \\
12-wide extensions & ROB 640, sched 448, 18 ports & Ph-1, Pc-1 \\

IPC (12-wide) & 4.8 target & Lion Cove class \\

%=====================================================================
\part{The Three Dies}
%=====================================================================

This part specifies the three die designs (Phone, PC, DC) that form the basis of the three Tier-1 products. For each die, it states the area breakdown by functional block, the die's physical configuration, the package specification, and the thermal design.

Every parameter required to tape out the die is stated.

\section{Die Overview}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{1.6cm}p{2cm}p{2.5cm}p{1.8cm}p{2.2cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Die} & \textbf{Area} & \textbf{Cores (physical)} & \textbf{Products} & \textbf{Package} & \textbf{Cooling} & \textbf{Peak / TDP} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Die} & \textbf{Area} & \textbf{Cores (physical)} & \textbf{Products} & \textbf{Package} & \textbf{Cooling} & \textbf{Peak / TDP} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Phone & 150~mm$^2$ & 6 big (12-wide) + 4 little & Ph-1 & FCBGA 16$\times$16 & Vapor chamber & 32/18 W \\
PC & 292~mm$^2$ & 36 $\times$ 12-wide & Pc-1 & LGA 1700 & 280~mm AIO & 234/182 W \\
DC & 205.9~mm$^2$ & 32 $\times$ 10-wide & Dc-1S & LGA 4677 & Air 1U & 206/175 W \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The three dies share the same FeFET cell, the same FeFET back-end module, the same NVL3 readout circuit, and the same CPU core family (RV64GCVH, 10-wide or 12-wide). The differences are in core count, core width, cache sizes, crossbar presence, memory interface, and I/O.

\subsection{Competitive positioning}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}}

\endhead
ST GIPS & 18.4 & 18.4 & 25.0 & 24.0 \\
MT GIPS & 74 & ~40 & ~70 & ~80 \\
AI 2-bit raw & 2.62e13 & ~2e13 & ~2e13 & ~0.9e13 \\
Peak power & 32~W & ~15~W & ~20~W & ~12~W \\
Deep sleep & 0.5~mW & ~100~mW & ~80~mW & ~50~mW \\
Die size & 150~mm$^2$ & 130~mm$^2$ & 150~mm$^2$ & 110~mm$^2$ \\
Price (OEM) & \$70 & ~\$150 & ~\$200 & Apple internal \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\textbf{Wins:} matches Snapdragon 8 Gen 3 ST; wins on MT (1.9$\times$), AI (1.3$\times$ raw, comparable real-workload), price (47\% of SD 8 Gen 3 BOM), and deep-sleep power (200$\times$).

\textbf{Loses:} 26\% behind SD 8 Elite on ST; 23\% behind A18 Pro on ST.

Ph-1 matches the mid-tier flagship. It does not beat the top-tier phone chips on single-thread. It wins on multi-thread, AI, battery life, and price.

\section{Binning and Yield Economics}

\subsection{Phone die}

The Phone die is fully enabled in Ph-1: all 6 big + 4 little cores are active. There is no core-count binning. Every Ph-1 die runs at the same clock (4.5~GHz boost / 3.0~GHz sustained).

\subsection{PC die}

The PC die has 36 cores physically. Pc-1 enables 28 of them (8 disabled). The 8 disabled cores are distributed across the die to balance the thermal profile. The binning threshold for Pc-1 is at least 28 good cores out of the die's 36 physical cores.

At mature yield ($D_0$ = 0.1/cm$^2$), the PC die yield is 75.1\%. Dies that meet the 28-good-core threshold are used in Pc-1; the cost basis below uses the 75.1\% die yield.

\subsection{DC die}

The DC die is fully enabled in Dc-1S: all 32 cores are active. The binning threshold for Dc-1S is all 32 good cores.

The margin at mature yield is 21--50\% across the three products: 28.2\% for Ph-1, 21.2\% for Pc-1 (lower due to the larger die), 49.9\% for Dc-1S. The blended margin depends on the product mix.

%=====================================================================
\part{Fabrication}
%=====================================================================

This part specifies the fabrication flow that combines a standard N5 CMOS front-end with the FeFET middle-end-of-line (MEOL) back-end module. Both flows are proven independently at commercial foundries; combining them is an integration exercise with a defined acceptance criterion at each stage.

\section{Process Overview}

The platform uses:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Front-end:} standard N5 CMOS (FinFET). Standard library cells, standard PLLs, standard DDR5 PHY, standard PCIe PHY, standard SRAM compilers, standard IO cells. No new front-end process step.
\item \textbf{Back-end:} FeFET MEOL module. Bottom electrode (TiN), HZO (10~nm ALD), RTA 500~°C, top electrode, pillar patterning, disk etch, passivation, contacts, Cu interconnect, final anneal.
\end{itemize}

The CMOS and FeFET modules are joined at the MEOL, between M3 and M4 metal layers. Standard CMOS uses M0--M3 for local interconnect and M4--M14 for global. The FeFET module uses M\_Fe1 (bottom electrode) and M\_Fe2 (top electrode) inside the MEOL slot, then standard BEOL continues with M4--M14.

\section{Thermal Budget}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{4cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Thermal budget} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Step} & \textbf{Thermal budget} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
FEOL (front-end) & up to 1000~°C & Standard CMOS \\
MEOL (M0--M3) & up to 450~°C & TiN barrier, low-k \\
FeFET module & 500~°C / 30~s RTA & Spike anneal, not sustained \\
BEOL (M4--M14) & $\le$ 400~°C & Cu damascene \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The 500~°C spike anneal is compatible with the lower metals because the duration is 30~s, the lower metals (TiN, Co, W) have melting points above 1500~°C, the low-k dielectrics at M0--M3 are rated to ~450~°C sustained and ~550~°C spike, and this is the standard MEOL insertion pattern for embedded NVM (MRAM at 280~°C, RRAM at 300~°C, PCRAM at 400~°C). The FeFET's 500~°C is hotter than these but is still in the spike-anneal regime.

\section{The FeFET Back-End Module (14 Steps)}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Substrate prep.} Clean the N5 wafer after M3 planarization (standard RCA clean, HF dip, DI water rinse, N$_2$ dry).
\item \textbf{Bottom electrode.} TiN, 50~nm, ALD at 300~°C. Precursor: TiCl$_4$ + NH$_3$. Deposition rate 0.5~Å/cycle.
\item \textbf{HZO deposition.} 10~nm, ALD at 250~°C. HfO$_2$/ZrO$_2$ supercycle (Hf:Zr ratio 1:1) plus 1.5~nm SiO$_2$ interface. Precursors: Hf[N(CH$_3$)$_2$]$_4$, Zr[N(CH$_3$)$_2$]$_4$, O$_3$ as oxidant.
\item \textbf{RTA anneal.} 500~°C, 30~s, N$_2$ ambient. Spike anneal profile (ramp 100~°C/s, hold 30~s, cool 100~°C/s). This crystallizes HZO into the orthorhombic ferroelectric phase.
\item \textbf{Top electrode.} TiN, 10~nm, ALD at 300~°C.
\item \textbf{Top electrode patterning.} 193~nm immersion lithography. Two masks: FeFET\_A (210~nm $\times$ 130~nm) and FeFET\_B (420~nm $\times$ 130~nm) for the crossbar cell; single mask (210~nm $\times$ 130~nm) for the NVL3 cell.
\item \textbf{Disk etch.} Multi-step RIE (TiN + HZO + TiN). Endpoint detection via Hf emission at 313.5~nm. Over-etch 10\%.
\item \textbf{Post-etch anneal.} 400~°C, 30~min, O$_2$. This repairs etch damage to the HZO sidewalls.
\item \textbf{Passivation.} SiN (30~nm) + SiO$_2$ (200~nm), PECVD at 350~°C.
\item \textbf{Contact vias.} W, 50~nm, CVD. Two vias per cell (BL via, SL via).
\item \textbf{Cu via to M4.} Standard N5 Cu damascene. Connects the FeFET top electrode to the M4 routing layer.
\item \textbf{Standard BEOL continuation.} Top metal, pad.
\item \textbf{Final anneal.} Standard N5.
\item \textbf{Wafer sort.} Gates 1--4.
\end{enumerate}

\section{The 11-Mask FeFET Module}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{1.2cm}p{5cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Mask} & \textbf{Purpose} & \textbf{CD} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Mask} & \textbf{Purpose} & \textbf{CD} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
M\_fe1 & Bottom electrode & 360~nm & --- \\
M\_fe2 & HZO mesa (protect) & 400~nm & --- \\
M\_fe3 & Top electrode A & 210~nm & FeFET\_A (NVL3 and crossbar) \\
M\_fe4 & Top electrode B & 420~nm & FeFET\_B (crossbar only) \\
M\_fe5 & Pillar/disk & 150~nm & FeFET channel definition \\
M\_fe6 & Selector gate (WL\_sel) & 150~nm & NVL3 selector \\
M\_fe7 & FeFET gate (WL\_fe) & 130~nm & FeFET gate contact \\
M\_fe8 & BL via & 50~nm & Bit-line contact \\
M\_fe9 & SL via & 50~nm & Source-line contact \\
M\_fe10 & BL metal & 720~nm & M5 (bit line) \\
M\_fe11 & SL metal & 720~nm & M6 (source line) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

Total N5 masks: ~60 (standard). FeFET module: +11. Total: ~71 masks. The FeFET back-end adds ~15\% to mask cost.

For the Dc-1S die, which has no crossbar, M\_fe4 is dropped. Total DC die masks: ~70.

\section{Wafer Cost}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{2.5cm}p{3cm}}
