0x4C & ADC\_TRIM & RW & OTP & ADC trim (INL, DNL) from OTP \\
0x50 & DAC\_TRIM & RW & OTP & DAC trim (offset, gain) from OTP \\
0x54 & TIA\_TRIM & RW & OTP & TIA trim (offset, bandwidth) from OTP \\
0x58 & IRQ\_ENABLE & RW & 0 & [0] MAC\_DONE; [1] W\_UPDATE; [2] CALIB \\
0x5C & IRQ\_STATUS & RO & 0 & Mirrors IRQ\_ENABLE bit layout; latched until IC write \\
0x60 & IC & W1C & --- & Clear bits in IRQ\_STATUS \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

Weight update sequence: write W\_SRC\_ADDR (weight pattern in SRAM), W\_LEN, W\_ROW, W\_COL; write W\_UPDATE\_CTRL.START=1; poll W\_UPDATE\_STATUS.BUSY or wait for W\_UPDATE interrupt; check W\_UPDATE\_STATUS.DONE and .VERIFIED. The controller programs FeFET\_A and FeFET\_B in the row, applies read-verify per cell, retries up to 3 times.

Calibration: write CALIB\_COL and optionally CALIB\_GAIN\_COARSE / CALIB\_GAIN\_FINE / CALIB\_OFFSET overrides; write CALIB\_CTRL.START=1; poll CALIB\_STATUS.DONE. Factory calibration cycles all columns and stores the resulting per-column gain/offset in OTP. At boot, write CALIB\_CTRL.LOAD\_FROM\_OTP=1 to restore.

Option A/B mode: at boot, firmware reads the product's config from OTP (or from an ID register not shown here) and writes CTRL.W+/W−-MODE appropriately. On Ph-1 (single crossbar), mode=0 (time-multiplexed); on Pc-1 (parallel pair), mode=1.

%=====================================================================
\part*{Appendix B: Firmware Interface Specification}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendix B: Firmware Interface Specification}
%=====================================================================

This appendix specifies the protocols between firmware and hardware for the sequences that the register map alone does not fully define: NVL3 row write-verify, NVL3 pre-wake-up, crossbar weight update, crossbar calibration, firmware update, security engine key provisioning, and error recovery.

Every sequence is stated as a step-numbered procedure. Every register mentioned is defined in Appendix A. Timing budgets are stated where the sequence has a hard real-time constraint.

\section{Conventions}

\textbf{Status polling:} where the text says "poll STATUS.X", firmware reads STATUS in a loop until the named bit is set or a timeout (default 100 ms) expires. Timeout handling: escalate to the error recovery sequence at §B.8.

\textbf{Interrupt alternative:} wherever a status-poll is described, firmware may enable the corresponding IRQ\_ENABLE bit and service the interrupt. The protocols below state the polling form because it is the simplest; interrupt-driven versions follow the same register writes but replace the poll with an interrupt handler.

\textbf{Ordering:} all register writes to a single block must be separated by at least one `fence` instruction to ensure MMIO ordering. Writes to different blocks have no ordering constraint unless stated.

\textbf{Memory barriers:} where a register write triggers a DMA transfer, the source buffer must be flushed to memory (or marked non-cacheable) before the trigger. This is the standard RISC-V `fence rw, rw` before writing the trigger register.

\section{NVL3 Row Write-Verify}

Purpose: program a single 512-bit row of the NVL3 with a 512-bit data pattern, with automatic read-verify and retry.

\textbf{Preconditions:} NVL3 controller enabled (CTRL.ENABLE=1); the row is not currently in use as NVL3 cache; the caller has exclusive access to the bank (typically the NVL3 driver, during a cache line eviction or a boot-time firmware write).

\textbf{Procedure:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Write ROW\_ADDR with the target row (0--4095).
\item Write BANK\_ADDR with the target bank (0--255 for Ph-1/Pc-1, 0--95 for Dc-1S).
\item Write DATA\_LOW (8×32-bit words, 0x10--0x2F) with the low 256 bits of the row.
\item Write DATA\_HIGH (8×32-bit words, 0x30--0x4F) with the high 256 bits of the row.
\item Write WRITE\_VERIFY\_CTRL to set max-retries (default 3) and pulse width (default 25 ns).
\item Write CTRL.START\_WRITE\_VERIFY=1. This triggers the row erase + program + read-verify sequence.
\item Poll STATUS.BUSY until it clears (typical 400 ns, max 1.2 µs).
\item Read WRITE\_VERIFY\_STATUS.SUCCESS. If 1, proceed to step 9. If 0, read WRITE\_VERIFY\_STATUS.attempt\_count and STATUS.ERROR; then either retry (re-write CTRL.START\_WRITE\_VERIFY=1) or invoke error recovery (§B.8).
\item Read ECC\_STATUS. If SINGLE\_BIT=1, log the address (ECC\_ADDR) and clear via IC.SINGLE\_BIT=1. If DOUBLE\_BIT=1, mark the row bad (BAD\_BLOCK) and return error.
\item Return success.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Timing:} 400 ns nominal for a single-iteration write; up to 1.2 µs worst-case (4 iterations). The controller performs the verify read back from the FeFET cell, not from cache.

\textbf{Interrupt path:} enable IRQ\_ENABLE.VERIFY=1; the handler reads WRITE\_VERIFY\_STATUS and STATUS and follows the same disposition logic.

\textbf{Cache interaction:} this sequence writes directly to NVL3. If the target row is currently cached in SRAM L3 or L2, the caller must invalidate those lines before starting (or accept that the next access will be a miss and re-fill from NVL3).

\section{NVL3 Pre-Wake-Up}

Purpose: apply the HZO polarization wake-up pulse sequence to a bank or to the full NVL3, so that the persistent retention target is met.

\textbf{Preconditions:} the target region is currently erased (all zeros) or can be treated as uninitialized. Pre-wake-up writes a dummy pattern, so any prior data is lost.

\textbf{Procedure (per bank):}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Write BANK\_ADDR.
\item Write PRE\_WAKEUP\_CTRL with the pulse count. Factory default: 1000 pulses for the NVL3 data region, 10000 for the microcode region. Write 0x3E8 for data, 0x2710 for microcode.
\item Write CTRL.START\_PRE\_WAKEUP=1.
\item Poll PRE\_WAKEUP\_STATUS.DONE. The controller applies the pulse train in a single sweep; typical time 1.3 s for 1000 pulses (25 ns per pulse plus setup overhead), 13 s for 10000 pulses.
\item Read PRE\_WAKEUP\_STATUS.ERROR. If 1, error recovery; if 0, return success.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Timing:} 1.3 s per bank for 1000 pulses. The full NVL3 (256 banks for Ph-1/Pc-1, 96 for Dc-1S) takes ~333 s (Ph-1) or ~125 s (Dc-1S) at factory test. This is why pre-wake-up is done once at factory and not at every boot.

\textbf{After pre-wake-up:} the affected region must be re-initialized (factory test writes the microcode image and the initial NVL3 content) and re-calibrated (write CALIB\_START=1 and poll CALIB\_STATUS.DONE).

\section{NVL3 Calibration Load and Validation}

Purpose: on each cold boot, load the per-column calibration table from OTP into the controller's working SRAM, and validate that a known pattern reads back correctly with the loaded offsets.

\textbf{Preconditions:} OTP controller has been unlocked by the security engine; OTP calibration region is populated (factory-programmed).

\textbf{Procedure:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Write CALIB\_START=1. The controller reads the calibration table from OTP (base and length are OTP-programmed into CAL\_TABLE\_BASE and CAL\_TABLE\_LEN at factory).
\item Poll CALIB\_STATUS.DONE (typical 5 ms, max 20 ms).
\item Read CALIB\_STATUS.ERROR. If 1, error recovery (§B.8).
\item Validation: write a known pattern to bank 0, row 0; read back; compare; if mismatch, error recovery.
\item If validation succeeds, return success. The NVL3 controller is now ready to serve the data window at 0x8000\_0000.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Boot path requirement:} this sequence must complete before any NVL3 access, including firmware image load. Boot ROM calls it as its first action after PLL lock.

\section{Crossbar Weight Update}

Purpose: program a contiguous run of weight cells in the analog crossbar to a new pattern (per-model weight update).

\textbf{Preconditions:} crossbar controller enabled (CTRL.ENABLE=1); the target cells are currently unused or are being re-purposed; the new pattern is in SRAM at a firmware-controlled address.

\textbf{Procedure:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Write W\_SRC\_ADDR (low) and W\_SRC\_ADDR\_HI (high) with the SRAM address of the new weight pattern. The pattern is a contiguous array of 2-bit (or 4-bit or 8-bit, in the AO configurations) weight values, packed according to the current VECMODE.
\item Write W\_LEN with the number of cells to update. Maximum per call: 512 (one row of a 512² crossbar) or 1024 (one row of a 1024² crossbar).
\item Write W\_ROW with the target row.
\item Write W\_COL with the target column start.
\item Write W\_UPDATE\_CTRL.START=1, .VERIFY\_EN=1, .HALT\_ON\_ERR=0 (or 1 if the caller can tolerate a partial update).
\item Poll W\_UPDATE\_STATUS.BUSY (typical 38 µs for a 512-cell row on Ph-1, 77 µs for a 1024-cell row on Pc-1; these are worst-case values from §5.12).
\item Read W\_UPDATE\_STATUS.DONE and .VERIFIED. If VERIFIED=1, the update succeeded for all cells. If VERIFIED=0, read W\_VERIFY (the value of the last cell) and the source data at the corresponding offset; identify the first failing cell and either retry (re-write the failing segment) or invoke error recovery.
\item Return success.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Ordering:} multiple weight updates must be serialized (wait for one to complete before starting another). Firmware enforces this via a mutex in the AI driver.

\textbf{Inference suspension:} during a weight update, inference must be suspended (no `crossbar.macc` issued). The AI driver enforces this via its own scheduling; the hardware does not enforce it, and issuing MAC during an update produces undefined results.

\section{Crossbar Calibration}

Purpose: measure and store the per-column gain, offset, and temperature coefficient of the crossbar, so that the separable model in §5.9 has valid coefficients.

\textbf{Procedure (per column):}

\begin{enumerate}
\item For each of the four known weight states (00, 01, 10, 11), program the entire column with that weight (W\_UPDATE with W\_ROW=0, W\_COL=all, W\_LEN=all).
\item For each of the 16 DAC codes, apply the DAC code as the input to the entire column, read the ADC output for each row, and compute the column-averaged current.
\item The result is a 4 × 16 table of (weight\_state, dac\_code) → column\_average\_current.
\item Fit the separable model: gain = slope of (current vs weight\_state at fixed DAC); offset = intercept; tempco = the difference between the 25°C and 85°C fits divided by 60°C.
\item Store the fitted parameters in OTP via the OTP controller (Appendix A §A.10). Per column: 2 bits weight-state-major + 20 bits gain + 2 bits tempco per the schema in §5.9.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Time:} approximately 20 minutes per die for a 1024² crossbar at factory test; this is the dominant crossbar factory test time. The full procedure is described in the Crossbar Test Program (separate artifact).

\textbf{Boot path:} at cold boot, firmware writes CALIB\_CTRL.LOAD\_FROM\_OTP=1 and polls CALIB\_STATUS.OTP\_LOADED; this restores the fitted coefficients into the working registers.

\section{Firmware Update (Dual-Bank A/B)}

Purpose: atomically replace the running firmware with a new signed image, with rollback on failure.

\textbf{Preconditions:} new image is signed by the OEM ECDSA P-256 key and has a version number greater than the current running version (rollback protection).

\textbf{Procedure:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Firmware reads the new image into SRAM (from host, SPI, or storage) and verifies its ECDSA P-256 signature against the OEM public key in OTP.
\item If verification fails, abort and log.
\item If verification succeeds, firmware writes the new image to the backup NVL3 bank. The backup bank is the one that firmware is NOT currently running from (A/B alternation).
\item After the write completes and read-verifies, firmware writes the new image's version number to the OTP rollback counter area, using the OTP controller.
\item Firmware writes the boot pointer in OTP to point at the backup bank, then resets.
\item On the next boot, Boot ROM reads the boot pointer, verifies the selected bank's signature, and jumps to it.
\item If Boot ROM fails to verify or if the kernel fails to reach userspace within a watchdog timeout (typically 60 s), Boot ROM reverts the boot pointer to the previous bank and resets. The previous bank is retained for exactly this purpose.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Physical presence:} unsigned recovery images (for RMA only) require the physical-presence button to be held during reset. The button state is read via a GPIO input; firmware enforces the policy.

\textbf{Anti-rollback:} firmware compares the new image's version number to the OTP rollback counter. A newer version always wins; a same-or-older version is rejected unless physical presence is asserted.

\section{Security Engine Key Provisioning}

Purpose: install the device-unique ECDSA key, the OEM root key hash, and the debug public key into OTP at factory test. This is the only time these values are written; they are then locked.

\textbf{Preconditions:} OTP is unlocked (LOCK\_STATUS=0); the security engine has authenticated the factory programmer via a factory-level challenge/response (separate flow, not described here); the keys are provided as files by the OEM.

\textbf{Procedure:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item Write REGION\_BASE=0 (root key hash region).
\item Write ADDR=0, DATA\_WRITE=first 32 bits of root key hash, CTRL.PROG\_ENABLE=1, PROG\_STROBE=1.
\item Poll STATUS.PROG\_DONE (typical 100 µs per word; a 32 B hash is 8 words, ~800 µs total).
\item Repeat for the full root key hash (32 B = 8 words).
\item Repeat the read-verify for each word (READ\_STROBE, compare DATA\_READ to DATA\_WRITE).
\item Write REGION\_BASE=1 (device key region). Repeat with the 32 B device ECDSA private key.
\item Write REGION\_BASE=2 (debug public key region). Repeat with the 32 B debug public key.
\item Write REGION\_BASE=3 (rollback counter region). Initialize the counter to 1.
\item Write REGION\_BASE=4 (boot nonce region). Initialize the nonce to a random 16 B value generated by the TRNG.
\item For each region, write LOCK=1 and poll LOCK\_STATUS until the region shows locked.
\item The security engine now enforces that OTP writes to these regions fail (STATUS.LOCKED=1) and that the security boot flow uses the provisioned values.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Failure handling:} if any read-verify fails (DATA\_READ ≠ DATA\_WRITE), the device is scrapped. There is no retry on OTP programming. This is why the security provisioning step is done last, after all other factory tests pass.

\textbf{Backup key handling:} the OEM's factory test program holds the keys in memory during provisioning and zeroizes them on completion. The keys are not stored on the factory test system between runs.

\section{Error Recovery}

Purpose: given any error status from any block, restore the system to a defined safe state and, where possible, recover without a reset.

\textbf{NVL3 verify failed:} read WRITE\_VERIFY\_STATUS; if attempt count < max-retries, retry (re-write CTRL.START\_WRITE\_VERIFY=1). If max-retries exhausted, mark the row bad: write BAD\_BLOCK (increment count), write BAD\_BLOCK\_ADDR=current row; the NVL3 driver then excludes the row from use and remaps to a redundant row (software-level remapping; the hardware does not auto-repair). Return error to caller.

\textbf{NVL3 ECC double-bit:} read ECC\_ADDR; read back the row from a redundant copy (if a redundant copy exists) or from the source of truth (SRAM or DDR5); log the address; mark the row bad as above.

\textbf{NVL3 endurance warning:} read WEAR\_COUNT\_LOW/HIGH; if the count exceeds the endurance threshold (default: 50% of the target endurance from §3.10), stop using the cell for persistent writes and direct writes to DDR5 or NVMe. The NVL3 driver enforces this per-bank.

\textbf{Crossbar weight update failed:} read W\_VERIFY and compare against the source; retry the failing segment. If 3 consecutive retries fail, mark the row bad (log for factory RMA) and continue with the rest of the model.

\textbf{Crossbar calibration failed:} read CALIB\_STATUS; if ERROR=1, the calibration table in OTP is corrupt or the on-die reference is out of range. Retry once; if it still fails, boot without crossbar (set VECMODE=00 idle) and log the fault.

\textbf{Thermal trip:} read THROTTLE and the trip sensor's raw count; if thermal shutdown is asserted, no software recovery is possible; the PMIC shuts the rails down. Firmware must not attempt to re-enable. On next boot, read the trip sensor's STATUS.TRIP\_LATCHED to see the cause.

\textbf{Watchdog reset:} on next boot, read RESET\_CAUSE. If RS:WDT, the firmware that was running is suspect; boot into a safe recovery image (a separate signed image at a fixed OTP address) that logs the incident, then reboots to the primary image. If the safe image also fails, boot into the factory diagnostic image (physical presence required).

\textbf{PCIe/CXL link error:} read LINK\_ERROR; write LINK\_ERROR\_CLR; if the error recurs within 100 ms, force a retrain (write LINK\_CTRL.FORCE\_RETRAIN=1) and wait for LINK\_STATUS.UP. If retrain fails 3 times, fall back to reduced speed (write SPEED=0x20 or 0x10) and continue.

\textbf{Brown-out:} read BOD\_STATUS to identify which rail dropped; report to BMC (Pc-1, Dc-1S) or log to NVL3 (Ph-1); do not attempt to continue until the rail is stable.

\section{Summary Table: Firmware Sequences}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2cm}p{3cm}p{4.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Sequence} & \textbf{When} & \textbf{Typical time} & \textbf{Failure action} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Sequence} & \textbf{When} & \textbf{Typical time} & \textbf{Failure action} \\
\midrule
\endhead
NVL3 row write-verify & On cache eviction or explicit write & 400 ns & Retry up to 3, then mark row bad \\

This appendix assigns the PLIC-lite interrupt sources. 16 sources are available on Ph-1; 64 on Pc-1 and Dc-1S. Sources 0--15 are common to all products; sources 16--63 exist only on Pc-1 and Dc-1S.

\section{Priority Encoding}

Priority values are 0--7 (3-bit priority, matching the PLIC-lite 4-bit priority field with the top bit reserved). 0 = disabled (source cannot interrupt). 7 = highest. Firmware may override the default priorities at boot by writing PLIC PRIORITY[n]. The defaults above are chosen so that the highest-severity sources (brown-out, watchdog, thermal trip, NMI, pipeline lockstep mismatch) are 7; the "attention" sources (ECC double-bit, security health fail) are 6; the "log-and-continue" sources are 3--5; the "informational" sources (DMA complete, PMU overflow) are 2--3.

\section{Per-Product Differences}

\textbf{Ph-1:} sources 16--63 are not implemented. Crossbar and CXL sources do not exist. Sources 34--47 are also not present (SRAM/caches are the same, but no separate register file parity interrupt on the 12-wide phone core — the parity is checked but not latched to PLIC). The PLIC-lite on Ph-1 has 16 usable sources (0--15, with 0 reserved, so 15 usable).

\textbf{Pc-1:} all 64 sources implemented.

\textbf{Dc-1S:} all 64 sources implemented. Crossbar sources 16--18 read as always-zero (no crossbar on Dc-1S), but the lines exist and are valid to enable — they simply never fire.

\section{Interrupt Handler Ordering}

When multiple sources are pending, the PLIC-lite serves them in strict priority order (highest priority first; ties broken by lowest source number). Firmware may reorder by writing PRIORITY.

For latency-critical sources (brown-out, watchdog), firmware should also configure the watchdog's own RESET\_EN bit as a hardware backstop: if firmware does not service the watchdog interrupt within the TIMEOUT window, the watchdog resets the chip. This is the standard two-level defense (interrupt for logging, reset for safety).

\section{NMI Configuration}

Source 63 (NMI) is the aggregated non-maskable interrupt. It fires when any of the following occurs: thermal trip (11), pipeline lockstep mismatch (44), or brown-out (12). The NMI handler in firmware must immediately:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Read all trip-status registers (THERMAL.STATUS, BOD\_STATUS, pipeline parity) to identify the cause.
\item Log to NVL3 (if available) or to BMC (if available).
\item Initiate the safe-state sequence: stop all DMA, quiesce the crossbar, halt user cores, and prepare for reset.
\item Trigger watchdog FORCE\_RESET=1 if the safe-state sequence does not complete within 100 ms.
\end{enumerate}

The NMI cannot be masked by PLIC settings. It is guaranteed to fire on the next cycle after the event.

%=====================================================================
\part*{Appendix D: Device Tree and ACPI Bindings}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendix D: Device Tree and ACPI Bindings}
%=====================================================================

This appendix specifies the platform description data that firmware presents to the operating system. The design supports two boot-time platform description modes: Device Tree (DT), standard for Linux and Android on RISC-V; and ACPI, standard for NUMA-aware server Linux, CXL-aware operating systems, and other ACPI-dependent software. The two are mutually exclusive at boot; the boot ROM selects one based on an OTP configuration bit and passes the corresponding pointer to the OS.

This appendix specifies:

\begin{itemize}
\item The boot modes and how firmware chooses between them.
\item The Device Tree bindings for every custom block (compatible strings, node structure, required and optional properties).
\item The ACPI table inventory and the per-product values that populate each table.
\item The DSDT namespace for every custom block (full ASL definitions).
\item The boot handoff contract (registers, memory state, state transitions).
\item The validation procedure that verifies the tables are correct before tape-out.
\end{itemize}

The standard ACPI table formats (RSDP, XSDT, MADT, SRAT, SLIT, MCFG, SPCR, GTDT, RHCT, IORT, CEDT, PPTT) are defined in ACPI~6.5 and are not repeated here; this appendix gives the design-specific values and the DSDT namespace. The standard DT bindings for RISC-V (PLIC, CLINT, CPU, memory) are defined in the Linux kernel documentation; this appendix gives the design-specific nodes and properties.

\section{Boot Modes}

Two boot modes are supported. The mode is selected by an OTP bit (OTP region~4, word~0, bit~0), programmed at factory test per the OEM's order.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{When used} & \textbf{OS-side consequence} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Mode} & \textbf{When used} & \textbf{OS-side consequence} \\
\midrule
\endhead
DT & Linux direct boot; Android boot & RISC-V Linux reads FDT from \texttt{a1}; Android reads FDT from \texttt{a1}; no ACPI tables loaded \\
ACPI & Windows-via-Wine under Linux; NUMA/CXL-aware server Linux & RISC-V Linux reads ACPI tables via UEFI system table or via RSDP pointed to by a special DT node; OS uses ACPI for enumeration \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

Default: DT. ACPI mode is opt-in for server-class configurations where NUMA and CXL topology need OS-level enumeration.

\section{Device Tree Bindings}

The DT is passed to the OS as a Flattened Device Tree (FDT) blob. The blob is generated by the boot ROM from a static DTS template compiled at build time, patched with per-product parameters (memory size, sensor count, crossbar presence, CXL presence) that are read from OTP.

\subsection{Vendor prefix and naming}

Custom compatible strings use the vendor prefix \texttt{ferrohybrid,} (lowercase, no capital, no space). The prefix is a descriptive namespace, not a company name. Standard RISC-V bindings (\texttt{riscv,}, \texttt{sifive,}, \texttt{riscv,plic0}, etc.) are used for standard components.

\subsection{Root node and chosen}

\begin{verbatim}
/ {
    #address-cells = <2>;
    #size-cells = <2>;
    model = "Ferroelectric Hybrid Processor Family";
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,pc-1", "ferrohybrid,fhpf";
    // patched at build time to one of:
    //   "ferrohybrid,ph-1", "ferrohybrid,pc-1", "ferrohybrid,dc-1s"

    chosen {
        bootargs = "console=ttySIF0,115200 earlycon=sbi";
        stdout-path = "/soc/serial@40005000";
        riscv,isa-base = "rv64";
        riscv,isa-extensions = "i,m,a,f,d,c,g,v,h";
    };

    cpus {
        #address-cells = <1>;
        #size-cells = <0>;
        // populated per product (see cpus node below)
    };

    memory@80000000 {
        device_type = "memory";
        reg = <0x0 0x80000000 0x0 0x0>;  // size patched per product
    };

    soc {
        #address-cells = <2>;
        #size-cells = <2>;
        compatible = "simple-bus";
        ranges;

        // all peripheral nodes below
    };
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{CPU nodes}

The \texttt{cpus} node enumerates every hart. Ph-1 has 10 harts (6 big + 4 little); Pc-1 has 28 harts; Dc-1S has 32 harts. Each hart gets a \texttt{cpu@<hart-id>} node with a \texttt{cpu-map} entry.

\begin{verbatim}
cpus {
    #address-cells = <1>;
    #size-cells = <0>;

    cpu@0 {
        device_type = "cpu";
        compatible = "riscv";
        reg = <0>;
        status = "okay";
        riscv,isa = "rv64imafdcvh";
        mmu-type = "riscv,sv39";
        clock-frequency = <6200000000>;  // patched per product / bin
        cpu-idle-states = <&CPU_SLEEP_0 &CPU_SLEEP_1>;

        interrupt-controller {
            #interrupt-cells = <1>;
            compatible = "riscv,cpu-intc";
            interrupt-controller;
        };
    };

    // ... one cpu@N per hart, up to the product's hart count

    cpu-map {
        cluster0 {
            core0 { cpu = <&cpu0>; };
            core1 { cpu = <&cpu1>; };
            // ...
        };
        // Ph-1 has two clusters: cluster0 (big) and cluster1 (little)
    };

    idle-states {
        CPU_SLEEP_0: cpu-sleep-0 {
            compatible = "riscv,idle-state";
            riscv,sbi-sleep-type = <1>;  // WFI
            entry-latency-us = <10>;
            exit-latency-us = <10>;
            min-residency-us = <100>;
        };
        CPU_SLEEP_1: cpu-sleep-1 {
            compatible = "riscv,idle-state";
            riscv,sbi-sleep-type = <2>;  // P3 deep sleep
            entry-latency-us = <10>;
            exit-latency-us = <10000>;   // 10 µs wake
            min-residency-us = <1000000>;
        };
    };
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Interrupt controller (PLIC-lite)}

The PLIC-lite is a variant of the standard RISC-V PLIC (16 or 64 sources; single hart). The DT node declares it with the standard \texttt{riscv,plic0} compatible string plus a design-specific qualifier.

\begin{verbatim}
plic: interrupt-controller@40002000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,plic-lite", "riscv,plic0";
    reg = <0x0 0x40002000 0x0 0x1000>;
    #interrupt-cells = <1>;
    interrupt-controller;
    riscv,ndev = <64>;         // 16 on Ph-1
    interrupts-extended = <&cpu0_intc 11>;
    // The single hart that services all PLIC interrupts
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{CLINT}

Standard RISC-V CLINT at the standard address.

\begin{verbatim}
clint: timer@40001000 {
    compatible = "riscv,clint0";
    reg = <0x0 0x40001000 0x0 0x10000>;
    interrupts-extended = <&cpu0_intc 3 &cpu0_intc 7>;
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Serial console}

The host interface block (Appendix~A §A.8) exposes a UART-like console on Ph-1. On Pc-1 and Dc-1S, the console is provided by the SoC's integrated UART (a standard 16550-compatible block added at 0x40005000 for this design).

\begin{verbatim}
serial@40005000 {
    compatible = "ns16550a";
    reg = <0x0 0x40005000 0x0 0x100>;
    clock-frequency = <1843200>;
    current-speed = <115200>;
    reg-shift = <2>;
    reg-io-width = <4>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 14>;
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{GPIO}

\begin{verbatim}
gpio@40000000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,gpio";
    reg = <0x0 0x40000000 0x0 0x100>;
    gpio-controller;
    #gpio-cells = <2>;
    interrupt-controller;
    #interrupt-cells = <2>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 20>;
    ngpios = <32>;             // 8 on Ph-1
    ferrohybrid,drive-strengths = <2 4 8 12>;  // mA
    ferrohybrid,pull-modes = "none", "up", "down", "keeper";
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Thermal sensors}

Each sensor is a separate node; the number of nodes is 4 (Ph-1), 6 (Pc-1), or 8 (Dc-1S).

\begin{verbatim}
thermal@40004000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,thermal";
    reg = <0x0 0x40004000 0x0 0x100>;
    #thermal-sensor-cells = <1>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 10 &plic 11>;
};

thermal-zones {
    cpu-thermal0 {
        polling-delay-passive = <100>;
        polling-delay = <1000>;
        thermal-sensors = <&thermal 0>;
        trips {
            cpu_alert: cpu-alert {
                temperature = <85000>;   // 85°C in millidegrees
                hysteresis = <5000>;
                type = "passive";
            };
            cpu_crit: cpu-crit {
                temperature = <115000>;  // 115°C in millidegrees
                hysteresis = <0>;
                type = "critical";
            };
        };
        cooling-maps {
            map0 { trip = <&cpu_alert>; cooling-device = <&cpu0 1 3>; };
        };
    };
    // ... additional zones per sensor
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{NVL3 controller}

\begin{verbatim}
nvl3: nvl3-controller@4000D000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,nvl3-controller";
    reg = <0x0 0x4000D000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 4 &plic 5 &plic 6 &plic 7 &plic 8>;
    // Single-bit ECC, double-bit ECC, verify, endurance, bad-block
    ferrohybrid,nvl3-size-mb = <64>;        // 24 for Dc-1S
    ferrohybrid,nvl3-banks = <256>;         // 96 for Dc-1S
    ferrohybrid,nvl3-window = <0x0 0x80000000 0x0 0x4000000>;  // 64 MB
    // ... additional properties for write-verify, pre-wake-up, endurance
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Crossbar controller (Ph-1, Pc-1 only)}

\begin{verbatim}
crossbar: crossbar-controller@4000E000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,crossbar-controller";
    reg = <0x0 0x4000E000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 16 &plic 17 &plic 18>;
    // MAC done, weight update, calibration
    ferrohybrid,crossbar-rows = <1024>;     // 512 on Ph-1
    ferrohybrid,crossbar-cols = <1024>;
    ferrohybrid,crossbar-bits = <2>;        // 2-bit baseline
    ferrohybrid,crossbar-mode = "parallel"; // "time-mux" on Ph-1
    ferrohybrid,custom-instructions = "0x0B";  // opcode space
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Security engine and debug}

\begin{verbatim}
security@4000A000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,security-engine";
    reg = <0x0 0x4000A000 0x0 0x200>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 21 &plic 22 &plic 23 &plic 24 &plic 25>;
    // AES done, SHA done, ECDSA done, TRNG health, DICE
};

debug@4000B000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,debug-auth";
    reg = <0x0 0x4000B000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 15>;
};

otp@40008000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,otp-controller";
    reg = <0x0 0x40008000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 26 &plic 27>;
    // read complete, program failed
    ferrohybrid,word-size = <4>;            // 32-bit words
    ferrohybrid,total-bytes = <1048576>;    // 1 MB
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{DMA, vector block, power, watchdog}

\begin{verbatim}
dma@40006000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,dma";
    reg = <0x0 0x40006000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 13>;
    dma-channels = <1>;
    #dma-cells = <1>;
    ferrohybrid,chunk-size = <4096>;
};

vector@40007000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,vector-block";
    reg = <0x0 0x40007000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 19>;
    status = "okay";                        // "disabled" on Dc-1S
};

power@40009000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,power-mgmt";
    reg = <0x0 0x40009000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 12>;
    ferrohybrid,dvfs-p-states = <5>;
    ferrohybrid,rails = "core", "io", "analog", "pll", "mem", "aux";
};

watchdog@40003000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,watchdog";
    reg = <0x0 0x40003000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 9>;
    ferrohybrid,timeout-modes = <1 10 100 1000>;  // ms
    ferrohybrid,reset-enable;
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{CXL (Pc-1, Dc-1S only)}

\begin{verbatim}
cxl@4000C000 {
    compatible = "ferrohybrid,cxl-control";
    reg = <0x0 0x4000C000 0x0 0x100>;
    interrupts-extended = <&plic 28 &plic 29 &plic 30>;
    // link up, link down, link error
    ferrohybrid,cxl-version = "3.0";
    ferrohybrid,link-width = <16>;
    ferrohybrid,link-speed = <64>;         // GT/s
    status = "okay";                        // "disabled" on Ph-1
};
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Chosen node (boot handoff)}

The \texttt{chosen} node carries the boot arguments and the initrd location (if any), plus a design-specific property pointing at the runtime firmware services.

\begin{verbatim}
chosen {
    bootargs = "console=ttySIF0,115200 earlycon=sbi";
    stdout-path = "/soc/serial@40005000";
    linux,initrd-start = <0x0 0x0>;        // patched if initrd present
    linux,initrd-end = <0x0 0x0>;
    ferrohybrid,firmware-version = "1.0.0";
    ferrohybrid,boot-mode = "dt";           // or "acpi"
};
\end{verbatim}

\section{ACPI Tables}

ACPI mode presents the platform via ACPI tables. The boot ROM generates the tables at build time and places them in reserved memory at a fixed address. The OS discovers them via the UEFI system table (if UEFI boot) or via the RSDP pointer in the DT \texttt{chosen} node (the DT is still passed in ACPI mode for the initial handoff, but the OS ignores the peripheral nodes and uses ACPI instead).

\subsection{Table inventory}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{1.5cm}p{4cm}p{2.5cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Table} & \textbf{Purpose} & \textbf{Products} & \textbf{ACPI reference} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Table} & \textbf{Purpose} & \textbf{Products} & \textbf{ACPI reference} \\
\midrule
\endhead
RSDP & Root pointer (points to XSDT) & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.5 \\
XSDT & Extended System Description Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.8 \\
FADT & Fixed ACPI Description Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.9 \\
DSDT & Differentiated System Description Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.11 \\
MADT & Multiple APIC Description Table (RISC-V) & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.12 \\
GTDT & Generic Timer Description Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.24 \\
RHCT & RISC-V Hart Capabilities Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.37 \\
SPCR & Serial Port Console Redirection & All & Microsoft SPCR spec \\
SRAT & System Resource Affinity Table (NUMA) & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.16 \\
SLIT & System Locality Information Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.17 \\
PPTT & Processor Properties Topology Table & All & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.30 \\
MCFG & Memory Mapped Configuration space & Pc-1, Dc-1S & PCI Firmware spec \\
IORT & I/O Remapping Table & Pc-1, Dc-1S & ACPI~6.5 §5.2.33 \\
CEDT & CXL Early Discovery Table & Pc-1, Dc-1S & CXL~3.0 spec \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{FADT}

Follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.9. The design-specific values:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{2.5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Field} & \textbf{Ph-1} & \textbf{Pc-1} & \textbf{Dc-1S} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Field} & \textbf{Ph-1} & \textbf{Pc-1} & \textbf{Dc-1S} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Preferred PM profile & Mobile & Desktop & Enterprise Server \\
SCI interrupt & Source 12 (brown-out) & Source 12 & Source 12 \\
SCI interrupt mode & level & level & level \\
PM1a\_EVT\_BLK & OTP region 4 & OTP region 4 & OTP region 4 \\
PM1\_CNT\_LEN & 2 & 2 & 2 \\
FLAGS.CENTURY & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
FLAGS.RTC\_S4 & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
FLAGS.RESET\_REG\_SUP & 1 & 1 & 1 \\
RESET\_REG & 0x40009014 & 0x40009014 & 0x40009014 \\
RESET\_VALUE & 0xDEAD & 0xDEAD & 0xDEAD \\
ARM\_BOOT\_ARCH & 0 & 0 & 0 \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{MADT (RISC-V variant)}

Follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.12. Contains the following RISC-V-specific entries:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Type 0x18 (RISC-V Hart)}: one entry per hart. \texttt{ACPI Processor ID} = hart ID; \texttt{Hart ID} = hart ID; \texttt{Flags} bit 0 = 1 (hart enabled); \texttt{ACPI Processor UID} = hart ID.
\item \textbf{Type 0x1B (RISC-V PLIC)}: one entry for the PLIC-lite. \texttt{RISC-V PLIC ID} = 0; \texttt{Hardware ID} = 0x40002000; \texttt{Platform Interrupt Sources} = 64 (16 on Ph-1); \texttt{GSI Base} = 0; \texttt{Maximum Priority} = 7.
\item \textbf{Type 0x1C (RISC-V APLIC)}: not used in this design (single-hart PLIC; no MSI).
\item \textbf{Type 0x0D (RISC-V IMSIC)}: not used.
\item \textbf{Type 0x19 (RISC-V IMSIC per hart)}: not used.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{GTDT}

Follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.24. The generic timer comes from the CLINT (standard RISC-V mtime/mtimecmp). \texttt{CntControlBase} = 0x40001000 + 0x4000 (standard CLINT mtimecmp offset); \texttt{CntReadBase} = 0x40001000 + 0xBFF8 (standard CLINT mtime offset).

\subsection{RHCT}

Follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.37. Lists the RISC-V ISA extensions supported by every hart: i, m, a, f, d, c, g, v, h, zicsr, zifencei, zicntr, zihpm.

\subsection{SRAT and SLIT (NUMA)}

SRAT follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.16. Contains:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Processor Local APIC/SAPIC Affinity}: one entry per hart, \texttt{Proximity Domain} = the NUMA node that contains the hart.
\item \textbf{Memory Affinity}: one entry per contiguous memory range, \texttt{Proximity Domain} = the NUMA node that contains that memory.
\item \textbf{Generic Initiator Affinity}: one entry per CXL device (Pc-1, Dc-1S), \texttt{Proximity Domain} = the NUMA node closest to that CXL device.
\end{itemize}

Per-product NUMA topology:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{3cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{NUMA nodes} & \textbf{Contents} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Product} & \textbf{NUMA nodes} & \textbf{Contents} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ph-1 & 1 & All harts + all LPDDR5X \\
Pc-1 & 1 (single-socket) or 2 (2-socket via CXL) & Node 0 = local harts + local DDR5 + local NVL3; Node 1 = remote socket's harts + memory + NVL3 (via CXL) \\
Dc-1S & 1 (single-socket) or 2--4 (multi-socket via CXL) & Node 0 = local harts + local DDR5 + local NVL3; Nodes 1..N = remote sockets \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

SLIT follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.17. Distances: 10 (local) on the diagonal; 20 for 1-hop CXL (adjacent socket); 30 for 2-hop CXL (ring topology on multi-socket Dc); 40 for more distant paths (if any).

\subsection{MCFG, IORT, CEDT}

MCFG (Pc-1, Dc-1S): PCIe ECAM base at \texttt{0x0000_0003_0000_0000}; 16 buses per root complex; base bus = 0; segment = 0.

IORT (Pc-1, Dc-1S): one ITS-free IORT (RISC-V does not have a GIC ITS); one SMMU-v3 node per PCIe root complex; one RMR (Root Memory Region) per PCIe device for MSI doorbells.

CEDT (Pc-1, Dc-1S): follows CXL~3.0 §9.18. Contains:
\begin{itemize}
\item CHBS (CXL Host Bridge Structure): one entry per CXL host bridge; \texttt{Base HPA} = the CXL.mem target address of the host bridge.
\item CFMWS (CXL Fixed Memory Window Structure): one entry per memory window; describes the range of host physical addresses that map to CXL devices.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{PPTT}

Follows ACPI~6.5 §5.2.30. Describes the CPU topology (packages, clusters, cores). One socket per physical package; one node per cluster (4--6 per die); one node per core.

\section{DSDT Namespace}

The DSDT declares the custom devices in the ACPI namespace using ASL. Every device gets a \_HID (Hardware ID) that is a design-specific string, a \_CRS (Current Resource Settings) descriptor that describes its MMIO range and interrupt, and \_DSD (Device-Specific Data) properties that describe the design-specific parameters.

\subsection{Vendor ID}

The design-specific \_HID prefix is \texttt{FHP} (Ferroelectric Hybrid Processor). The full \_HID for each device is \texttt{FHP0001} through \texttt{FHP000E} (12 custom devices, as follows):

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{4cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{\_HID} & \textbf{Device} & \textbf{Compatible DT node (for reference)} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{\_HID} & \textbf{Device} & \textbf{Compatible DT node (for reference)} \\
\midrule
\endhead
FHP0001 & GPIO & \texttt{ferrohybrid,gpio} \\
FHP0002 & PLIC-lite & \texttt{ferrohybrid,plic-lite} \\
FHP0003 & Watchdog & \texttt{ferrohybrid,watchdog} \\
FHP0004 & Thermal & \texttt{ferrohybrid,thermal} \\
FHP0005 & Host interface & \texttt{ns16550a} (serial) or PCIe config \\
FHP0006 & DMA & \texttt{ferrohybrid,dma} \\
FHP0007 & Vector block & \texttt{ferrohybrid,vector-block} \\
FHP0008 & OTP & \texttt{ferrohybrid,otp-controller} \\
FHP0009 & Power management & \texttt{ferrohybrid,power-mgmt} \\
FHP000A & Security engine & \texttt{ferrohybrid,security-engine} \\
FHP000B & Debug auth & \texttt{ferrohybrid,debug-auth} \\
FHP000C & CXL control & \texttt{ferrohybrid,cxl-control} \\
FHP000D & NVL3 controller & \texttt{ferrohybrid,nvl3-controller} \\
FHP000E & Crossbar controller & \texttt{ferrohybrid,crossbar-controller} \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{DSDT source (ASL)}

The full DSDT for the Pc-1 product. The Ph-1 and Dc-1S versions differ only in the values of the numeric parameters and the presence of the crossbar (Ph-1) or CXL (Dc-1S). The differences are noted inline.

\begin{verbatim}
DefinitionBlock ("", "DSDT", 2, "FHP   ", "FHPPC1  ", 0x00000001)
{
    Scope (\_SB)
    {
        Device (GPIO) {
            Name (_HID, "FHP0001")
            Name (_UID, 0)
            Name (_CRS, ResourceTemplate () {
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x40000000, 0x100)
                Interrupt (ResourceConsumer, Level, ActiveHigh, Exclusive) { 20 }
            })
            Name (_DSD, Package () {
                ToUUID("daffd814-6eba-4d8c-8a91-bc9bbf4aa301"),
                Package () {
                    Package () { "ngpios", 32 },
                    Package () { "drive-strengths", Package () { 2, 4, 8, 12 } },
                    Package () { "pull-modes", Package () { "none", "up", "down", "keeper" } }
                }
            })
            Method (_STA) { Return (0x0F) }
        }

        Device (PLIC) {
            Name (_HID, "FHP0002")
            Name (_UID, 0)
            Name (_CRS, ResourceTemplate () {
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x40002000, 0x1000)
            })
            Name (_DSD, Package () {
                ToUUID("daffd814-6eba-4d8c-8a91-bc9bbf4aa301"),
                Package () {
                    Package () { "riscv,ndev", 64 },
                    Package () { "interrupts-extended", Package () { 0, 11 } }
                }
            })
            Method (_STA) { Return (0x0F) }
        }

        Device (WDT0) {
            Name (_HID, "FHP0003")
            Name (_UID, 0)
            Name (_CRS, ResourceTemplate () {
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x40003000, 0x100)
                Interrupt (ResourceConsumer, Level, ActiveHigh, Exclusive) { 9 }
            })
            Name (_DSD, Package () {
                ToUUID("daffd814-6eba-4d8c-8a91-bc9bbf4aa301"),
                Package () {
                    Package () { "timeout-modes-ms", Package () { 1, 10, 100, 1000 } },
                    Package () { "reset-enable", 1 }
                }
            })
            Method (_STA) { Return (0x0F) }
        }

        Device (TEMP) {
            Name (_HID, "FHP0004")
            Name (_UID, 0)
            Name (_CRS, ResourceTemplate () {
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x40004000, 0x600)   // 6 sensors × 0x100
                Interrupt (ResourceConsumer, Level, ActiveHigh, Exclusive) { 10, 11 }
            })
            Name (_DSD, Package () {
                ToUUID("daffd814-6eba-4d8c-8a91-bc9bbf4aa301"),
                Package () {
                    Package () { "num-sensors", 6 },
                    Package () { "sensor-offset", 0x100 }
                }
            })
            Method (_STA) { Return (0x0F) }
        }

        Device (NVL0) {
            Name (_HID, "FHP000D")
            Name (_UID, 0)
            Name (_CRS, ResourceTemplate () {
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x4000D000, 0x100)
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x80000000, 0x4000000)   // 64 MB NVL3 window
                Interrupt (ResourceConsumer, Level, ActiveHigh, Exclusive) { 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 }
            })
            Name (_DSD, Package () {
                ToUUID("daffd814-6eba-4d8c-8a91-bc9bbf4aa301"),
                Package () {
                    Package () { "nvl3-size-mb", 64 },
                    Package () { "nvl3-banks", 256 },
                    Package () { "nvl3-endurance-write-count", 215 },
                    Package () { "nvl3-retention-years", 10 }
                }
            })
            Method (_STA) { Return (0x0F) }
        }

        Device (XBAR) {
            Name (_HID, "FHP000E")
            Name (_UID, 0)
            Name (_CRS, ResourceTemplate () {
                Memory32Fixed (ReadWrite, 0x4000E000, 0x100)
                Interrupt (ResourceConsumer, Level, ActiveHigh, Exclusive) { 16, 17, 18 }
            })
            Name (_DSD, Package () {
                ToUUID("daffd814-6eba-4d8c-8a91-bc9bbf4aa301"),
                Package () {
                    Package () { "crossbar-rows", 1024 },
                    Package () { "crossbar-cols", 1024 },
                    Package () { "crossbar-bits", 2 },
                    Package () { "crossbar-mode", "parallel" }
                }
            })
            Method (_STA) { Return (0x0F) }
        }

        // ... additional devices (DMA, Vector, OTP, Power, Security, Debug, CXL)
        // follow the same structure; see the table above for _HID and resource
        // definitions
    }

    // Thermal zones declared at the root scope
    Scope (\_TZ)
    {
        ThermalZone (TZ00) {
            Method (_TMP, 0, Serialized) {
                Return (\_SB.TEMP.TEMP0)      // read sensor 0
            }
            Method (_CRT, 0, Serialized) { Return (388) }   // 115°C in 0.1K
            Method (_PSV, 0, Serialized) { Return (358) }   // 85°C in 0.1K
            Name (_TC1, 2)
            Name (_TC2, 5)
            Name (_TSP, 10)
        }
        // additional zones per sensor
    }

    // Processor objects (one per hart, matching MADT Type 0x18)
    Scope (\_SB)
    {
        Processor (CPU0, 0, 0x00000410, 0x06) {}
        Processor (CPU1, 1, 0x00000410, 0x06) {}
        // ... one per hart, up to the product's hart count
    }
}
\end{verbatim}

The crossbar \texttt{XBAR} device is only present on Ph-1 and Pc-1. The \_STA method on Dc-1S returns 0 (device not present).

The CXL device is only present on Pc-1 and Dc-1S; its \_CRS declares the link control block at 0x4000C000 and the interrupt sources 28--30.

\section{Boot Handoff Protocol}

\subsection{DT mode}

The boot ROM, after completing the firmware sequence (Appendix~G), passes control to the OS or bootloader with:

\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{a0} = boot hart ID (must be 0, the boot hart).
\item \texttt{a1} = physical address of the FDT blob in memory.
\item \texttt{pc} = entry point of the kernel or bootloader.
\item \texttt{mstatus.MIE} = 0 (interrupts disabled).
\item \texttt{satp} = 0 (paging off; kernel sets up its own).
\item All PLIC sources disabled; firmware does not leave any interrupt pending.
\item The FDT is at a 2~MB-aligned address in a memory region not claimed by the kernel.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{ACPI mode}

The boot ROM:

\begin{itemize}
\item Generates the ACPI tables at build time into a reserved memory region (typically 4~MB at the top of DDR5).
\item Sets up the RSDP pointer in the DT \texttt{chosen} node \texttt{ferrohybrid,acpi-rsdp} property.
\item Passes \texttt{a0} = 0, \texttt{a1} = FDT address (the DT still enumerates the boot hart and memory; the OS uses the RSDP to find the full ACPI tables and ignores the DT peripheral nodes).
\item The kernel detects the RSDP and switches to ACPI-based enumeration.
\end{itemize}

The \texttt{ferrohybrid,acpi-rsdp} DT property is a standard UINT64 in the \texttt{chosen} node; the OS-side kernel patch reads it and calls \texttt{acpi\_early\_init} with the RSDP address.

\subsection{Per-product differences}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Ph-1} & \textbf{Pc-1} & \textbf{Dc-1S} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Parameter} & \textbf{Ph-1} & \textbf{Pc-1} & \textbf{Dc-1S} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Default boot mode & DT & ACPI & ACPI \\
Hart count (MADT/DT) & 10 & 28 & 32 \\
NUMA nodes (SRAT) & 1 & 1--2 & 1--4 \\
CXL presence (CEDT) & no & yes & yes \\
Crossbar presence (XBAR) & yes & yes & no \\
DDR5 base & 0x0\_8000\_0000 & 0x0\_8000\_0000 & 0x0\_8000\_0000 \\
DDR5 size & patched per SKU & patched per SKU & patched per SKU \\
SPCR baud & 115200 & 115200 & 115200 \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Validation and Test}

\subsection{ACPI validation (pre-tape-out)}

The ACPI tables are generated by a build script from a machine-readable description (typically a YAML file per product). Before tape-out, the tables are validated:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{ACPI CA checker}: build the tables with the ACPI Component Architecture (ACPICA) compiler, run the \texttt{acpixtract -a} extractor, and run the ACPICA \texttt{acpiexec} validator. All tables must pass with zero errors and zero warnings except for documented product-specific allowances.
\item \textbf{Linux ACPI parser dry run}: run the tables through the Linux ACPI parser on a host x86 machine (the parser is architecture-independent; the OS will reject the tables on x86 because the MADT has RISC-V entries, but the parse itself must succeed without errors before the OS examines the architecture).
\item \textbf{Windows ACPI checker}: for OEM partners who will support Windows-on-Wine, run Microsoft's ACPI validation tool (ASL compiler and firmware test suite).
\item \textbf{Manual review}: the DSDT namespace is reviewed by hand against the register map in Appendix A, to confirm that every _CRS range and _DSD property matches the hardware.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{DT validation (pre-tape-out)}

The DT nodes are validated:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{dtc (device tree compiler)}: compile the DTS to DTB; require zero warnings from \texttt{dtc -W all}.
\item \textbf{dt-schema}: run the DT through the dt-schema validator (the Linux DT schema repository), which checks that every node's compatible string matches a known schema. Custom compatible strings (\texttt{ferrohybrid,*}) use the design-supplied schema files, which the design team maintains alongside the DTS.
\item \textbf{Linux DT parser dry run}: boot a Linux kernel with \texttt{earlycon} on QEMU using the DTB; the kernel must accept the DT without errors up to the point where real hardware is needed.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Hardware bring-up test (post-silicon)}

Once the first silicon returns, the boot ROM is patched with the real OTP values and the test sequence is:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Boot into a bare-metal firmware image that reads the DTB/ACPI tables and prints their parsed content to the UART.
\item Verify every node / table entry against the register map. Discrepancy stops the bring-up.
\item Boot into a real Linux kernel with the DT (DT mode). The kernel must reach userspace.
\item Boot into a real Linux kernel with the ACPI tables (ACPI mode). The kernel must reach userspace and \texttt{dmesg | grep -i acpi} must show the expected enumeration.
\item For server products, boot into a NUMA-enabled kernel and confirm \texttt{numactl --hardware} matches the expected topology.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Common failures}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Symptom} & \textbf{Common cause} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Symptom} & \textbf{Common cause} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Kernel panics at ACPI parse & RSDP checksum mismatch; check the \_CRS checksum in the DSDT \\
Kernel finds only 1 CPU & MADT Type 0x18 flags bit 0 is 0 for other harts; or DT \texttt{status} is "disabled" \\
NUMA topology empty & SRAT/SLIT missing or SRAT Proximity Domain values do not match SLIT \\
CXL not enumerated & CEDT missing or CHBS.Base HPA does not match the memory window \\
NVL3 not detected & DSDT NVL0 _CRS window does not match the register map, or DT \texttt{nvl3-window} is wrong \\
Crossbar driver probe fails & XBAR _HID missing or custom-instruction opcode mismatch \\
Serial console dead & SPCR baud rate or SPCR Base Address mismatch \\
Thermal zone does not throttle & \_TZ._TMP returns a constant or the sensor index is out of range \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The single most common bring-up error is a mismatch between the DSDT _CRS range and the actual hardware register map. The register map in Appendix A is the source of truth; the DSDT must match it exactly.

\section{Summary}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{4cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Artifact} & \textbf{Delivered with} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Artifact} & \textbf{Delivered with} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
DTS template & this appendix (structure) + design repo (full file) & Compiled at build time \\
ACPI tables & generated from product YAML at build time & DSDT/SSDT compiled via ACPICA \\
DSDT source (ASL) & this appendix (full source) & Hand-maintained per product \\
Product YAML & design repo & One per product \\
ACPI validation report & pre-tape-out & ACPICA + Windows checker \\
DT validation report & pre-tape-out & dtc + dt-schema \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The DSDT source in §D.4 is the authoritative design definition for ACPI mode. The DT node structure in §D.3 is the authoritative design definition for DT mode. Any firmware that boots the design in either mode must generate tables that conform to the corresponding structure in this appendix.

%=====================================================================
\part*{Appendix E: Full Pin Description}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendix E: Full Pin Description}
%=====================================================================

This appendix specifies the ball assignment, signal catalog, differential pair catalog, power/ground ball assignment, and critical signal placement for the three packages used in the three Tier-1 products. The complete per-ball mapping is provided as a companion CSV file (\texttt{ballmap\_<product>.csv}) that ships with this specification; this appendix gives the format specification, the signal catalog, and the placement rules needed to validate or regenerate that file.

\section{Ballmap CSV File Format}

Each product ships with a ballmap CSV file. The file is the authoritative per-ball assignment; this appendix defines its format and the design rules that generate it.

\subsection{File naming}

\texttt{ballmap\_ph1.csv}, \texttt{ballmap\_pc1.csv}, \texttt{ballmap\_dc1s.csv}.

\subsection{Column definitions}

The CSV has exactly these columns, in this order:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{2cm}p{3.5cm}p{6cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Column} & \textbf{Type} & \textbf{Example} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Column} & \textbf{Type} & \textbf{Example} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ball & string & A1 & Ball coordinate (row letter + column number) \\
NetName & string & VDD\_CORE & Net name as used on the board \\
SignalGroup & enum & POWER\_CORE & Signal group name from the catalog in §E.3 \\
Rail & enum & VDD\_CORE & Power rail (blank for signals) \\
Direction & enum & POWER & In, Out, Bidir, NC, POWER, GND \\
IOStandard & string & 1.0V\_CMOS & I/O standard \\
DiffPair & string & PCIe\_TX0 & Differential pair name (blank if single-ended) \\
Polarity & char & P or N & P or N (blank if single-ended) \\
Function & string & PCIe Gen5 TX lane 0 & Functional description \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

A sample of the first five rows (one per category):

\begin{verbatim}
Ball,NetName,SignalGroup,Rail,Direction,IOStandard,DiffPair,Polarity,Function
A1,VDD_CORE,VDD_CORE,VDD_CORE,POWER,1.0V,POWER,,,
A2,VSS,VSS,VSS,POWER,0V,GND,,,
A3,PCIe_TX0_P,PCIE,PCIe,Out,PCIe5,PCIE_TX0,P,PCIe Gen5 TX lane 0 positive
A4,PCIe_TX0_N,PCIE,PCIe,Out,PCIe5,PCIE_TX0,N,PCIe Gen5 TX lane 0 negative
A5,DDR5_CH0_DQ0,DDR5,DDR5,Bidir,DDR5_0V,DDR5_CH0_DQ0,P,DDR5 channel 0 DQ bit 0
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Validation rules}

Every ballmap CSV must satisfy:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Every ball coordinate in the package grid appears exactly once.
\item Every NetName appears on exactly the number of balls required by the catalog (§E.3).
\item Every DiffPair appears on exactly two balls (one P, one N).
\item Ball coordinate rows and columns must be contiguous (no gaps) except where the package depopulates corners or power/ground regions.
\end{enumerate}

The validation script (\texttt{ballmap\_validate.py}) is provided with the specification. It checks these rules and produces a report.

Every differential pair is listed with its net name and its pair members. The ballmap CSV assigns each pair member to a specific ball coordinate; this section lists the pairs themselves.

\subsection{DDR5 (Pc-1, Dc-1S)}

Per channel: 32 DQ (single-ended) + 4 DQS pairs (8 balls). 40 balls per channel. The DQS pairs are:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{3cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Byte lane} & \textbf{DQS pair (P, N)} & \textbf{DQ bits} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Byte lane} & \textbf{DQS pair (P, N)} & \textbf{DQ bits} \\
\midrule
\endhead
0 & DDR5\_CHn\_DQS0\_P, DDR5\_CHn\_DQS0\_N & DQ0--DQ7 \\
1 & DDR5\_CHn\_DQS1\_P, DDR5\_CHn\_DQS1\_N & DQ8--DQ15 \\
2 & DDR5\_CHn\_DQS2\_P, DDR5\_CHn\_DQS2\_N & DQ16--DQ23 \\
3 & DDR5\_CHn\_DQS3\_P, DDR5\_CHn\_DQS3\_N & DQ24--DQ31 \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The command/address (CA) and clock (CK) signals are handled by the DDR5 controller IP; they use single-ended CA in this design, not the differential CA of standard DDR5, to keep the ball count at 40 per channel. This is a deliberate ball-count optimization; the DDR5 controller IP supports both modes.

For Pc-1 (8 channels): 32 DQS pairs total.
For Dc-1S (4 channels): 16 DQS pairs total.

\subsection{LPDDR5X (Ph-1)}

Per channel: 40 balls. Same arrangement as DDR5 — 32 DQ + 4 DQS pairs per channel = 40 balls.

2 channels: 64 DQ balls + 8 DQS pairs (16 balls) = 80 balls total, matches the 80-ball LPDDR5X group.

8 DQS pairs.

\subsection{Camera MIPI (Ph-1)}

4-lane MIPI D-PHY, 8 diff pairs = 16 balls.

Lanes 0--3, each lane = 1 differential pair. The 8 pairs are the 4 data lanes (RX from the camera) plus 4 additional lanes (or 2 clock pairs + 4 data, etc.; the exact arrangement is in the ballmap CSV).

\subsection{Display MIPI (Ph-1)}

8 balls = 4 diff pairs. 4-lane DSI: 4 data pairs (or 3 data + 1 clock).

\subsection{5G Modem (Ph-1)}

16 balls = 8 diff pairs. M-PHY (MIPI M-PHY) is bidirectional multi-lane. 4-lane × 2 directions = 8 pairs.

\section{Power and Ground Ball Assignment}

Every power and ground ball is assigned to one rail (or VSS). The rails and their ball counts are listed in §E.3. The physical placement follows these rules:

\subsection{Placement rules}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{VSS interleaving:} VSS balls are interleaved with signal balls at a ratio of at least 1 VSS per 4 high-speed signal balls, and 1 VSS per 8 single-ended signal balls, to provide return-current paths.
\item \textbf{Power/ground rings:} VDD\_CORE balls are concentrated in the center of the package (for lowest PDN impedance to the die power grid). VSS balls form an outer ring and an inner interleave.
\item \textbf{Rail separation:} VDD\_IO, VDD\_ANALOG, and VDD\_PLL are in separate quadrants or contiguous blocks, physically separated from each other to prevent coupling.
\item \textbf{Analog isolation:} VDD\_ANALOG (crossbar, NVL3 readout) and VDD\_PLL are surrounded by VSS guard balls.
\item \textbf{High-speed signals:} PCIe, CXL, DDR5, and LPDDR5X signals are placed on the package edge closest to the corresponding PHY in the die floorplan.
\item \textbf{Spare balls:} Pc-1's 251 spare and Dc-1S's 1420 spare are all VSS-grounded.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{VDD\_CORE placement}

VDD\_CORE balls are physically clustered to form a low-inductance power delivery network. For Pc-1 (400 VDD\_CORE balls) and Dc-1S (900 VDD\_CORE balls), the balls form a contiguous block in the package center, with VSS interleaved at 1 VSS per 2 VDD\_CORE in the interior and 1 VSS per 1 VDD\_CORE at the boundary.

For Ph-1 (40 VDD\_CORE balls), the VDD\_CORE balls are arranged in a 5$\times$8 grid in the package center.

\subsection{VDD\_ANALOG placement}

VDD\_ANALOG balls are placed on the quadrant adjacent to the analog PHY (crossbar and NVL3 readout) in the die floorplan. On Pc-1, this is the bottom-left quadrant; on Ph-1, the top-right; on Dc-1S, the bottom-right.

The VDD\_ANALOG group is isolated by 2 VSS guard rings (one inner, one outer) to prevent noise coupling from the core and I/O rails.

\subsection{VDD\_PLL placement}

VDD\_PLL balls (4 on Ph-1, 8 on Pc-1, 16 on Dc-1S) are placed immediately adjacent to the analog PLL block in the die floorplan, with dedicated VSS return balls.

\section{Critical Signal Placement}

For high-speed signal routing, the ball coordinate regions are constrained as follows.

\subsection{PCIe Gen5 (Pc-1, Dc-1S)}

All PCIe Gen5 pairs are placed on the package bottom edge (rows near the edge, e.g., the last 4 rows). TX and RX pairs for a given lane are adjacent to each other (within 2 ball columns) to allow skew-matched routing on the board. Lane 0 is the leftmost, lane 7 (or 15) is the rightmost.

\subsection{CXL 3.0 (Pc-1, Dc-1S)}

All CXL pairs are placed on the package left edge, mirroring the PCIe layout. Lane 0 is the bottom-most, lane 7 is the top-most.

\subsection{DDR5 (Pc-1, Dc-1S)}

DDR5 channels are split between two edges:
\begin{itemize}
\item Channels 0--3: top edge, arranged left to right.
\item Channels 4--7: right edge, arranged top to bottom (Pc-1 only; Dc-1S has 4 channels only, all on the top edge).
\end{itemize}

Within each channel, the DQ byte lanes are grouped, the DQS pair for each byte lane is adjacent to the corresponding DQ group, and the CA signals are grouped near the DQS of byte lane 0.

\subsection{LPDDR5X (Ph-1)}

LPDDR5X channels are on the top edge (channel 0) and right edge (channel 1), both near the corresponding PHY in the die floorplan.

\subsection{MIPI (Ph-1)}

Camera MIPI is on the top edge (near the camera ISP), display MIPI on the right edge (near the display controller), 5G modem on the bottom edge (near the modem interface).

\subsection{Placement summary table}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2.5cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}p{2cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Interface} & \textbf{Ph-1 edge} & \textbf{Pc-1 edge} & \textbf{Dc-1S edge} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Interface} & \textbf{Ph-1 edge} & \textbf{Pc-1 edge} & \textbf{Dc-1S edge} \\
\midrule
\endhead
PCIe & bottom & bottom & bottom \\
CXL & (none) & left & left \\
DDR5 & (none) & top (0--3) + right (4--7) & top \\
LPDDR5X & top + right & (none) & (none) \\
Camera MIPI & top & (none) & (none) \\
Display MIPI & right & (none) & (none) \\
5G modem & bottom & (none) & (none) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{No-Connect Balls}

Balls that are not connected to any net inside the die are marked NC in the ballmap CSV, are physical balls on the package but are not bonded to any die pad, and are typically not connected on the board.

For the three packages:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{2cm}p{3cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Package} & \textbf{NC balls} & \textbf{Location} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Package} & \textbf{NC balls} & \textbf{Location} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Ph-1 & 0 & No NC balls (all 400 balls are assigned) \\
Pc-1 & 0 & No NC balls (all 1851 assigned; spare = 251 VSS) \\
Dc-1S & 0 & No NC balls (all 4677 assigned; spare = 1420 VSS) \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

All spare balls in Pc-1 and Dc-1S are tied to VSS (not floated), to provide additional PDN return paths. The ballmap CSV marks them as "SPARE" signal group, "GND" rail, "POWER" direction.

\section{Validation and Test}

\subsection{Ballmap validation}

Before tape-out, the ballmap CSV is validated against:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Signal catalog consistency}: the CSV ball counts per signal group match §E.3.
\item \textbf{Diff pair completeness}: every DiffPair name in the CSV has exactly 2 balls (one P, one N).
\item \textbf{Rail assignment}: every ball has either a Rail name or a Direction of NC.
\item \textbf{Coordinate uniqueness}: no ball coordinate appears twice.
\item \textbf{Contiguity}: the ball grid has no unexpected gaps.
\end{enumerate}

The validation script (\texttt{ballmap\_validate.py}) produces a report.

\subsection{Package assembly validation (post-silicon)}

After the first silicon returns, the package is validated:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{X-ray inspection}: confirms all 400/1851/4677 solder joints are present and well-formed.
\item \textbf{Cross-section}: confirms solder joint shape and standoff height.
\item \textbf{Electrical continuity}: confirms every signal ball is electrically connected to the die pad it is assigned to.
\item \textbf{Diff pair impedance measurement}: TDR measurement on representative diff pairs, confirming 85~$\Omega$ (PCIe) or 80~$\Omega$ (DDR5).
\item \textbf{PDN impedance measurement}: vector network analyzer sweep of the VDD\_CORE plane, confirming target impedance.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Board-level acceptance}

For a board design to be accepted, it must:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Reproduce the ballmap assignment exactly (no ball re-purposed).
\item Meet the diff pair impedance and trace length specifications.
\item Meet the DQ-to-DQS skew specification for all DDR5/LPDDR5X channels.
\item Meet the VDD\_CORE PDN impedance target (typically $<1$~m$\Omega$ from DC to 10~MHz).
\end{enumerate}

\part*{Appendix F: Mechanical Drawings}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendix F: Mechanical Drawings}
%=====================================================================

This appendix specifies the mechanical dimensions, tolerance stack-up, heatsink mounting, thermal interface, and board keep-out zones for the three packages. The mechanical drawing package (DXF, DWG, SVG, and PDF files) ships with this specification; this appendix gives the dimensions in tabular form.

\section{Package Outline Dimensions}

\subsection{FCBGA 16$\times$16 (Ph-1)}

\begin{center}

The package has a laser-marked orientation dot at A1 corner (0.5~mm diameter) on the lid top surface. The pin 1 indicator is a chamfered corner on the substrate (45$^\circ$ chamfer, 1.0~mm).

\subsection{LGA 1700 (Pc-1)}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{3cm}p{2cm}p{4cm}}

The package has an integrated heat spreader (IHS) with a stamped dimple for IHS-to-die thermal contact. The IHS is Ni-plated Cu, 1.3~mm thick, with a flatness of 0.05~mm over the TIM contact area.

The orientation marker is a triangular notch on the substrate edge at A1 corner (2.0~mm), and a laser-marked logo on the IHS top surface.

\section{Substrate Stack-Up}

\subsection{FCBGA 16$\times$16 substrate}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2.5cm}p{4.5cm}}

\endhead
L1 (top) & 15~$\mu$m Cu & Die attach + lid seal \\

\subsection{LGA 1700 substrate}

8-layer build-up substrate: 1-2-1 (one core, two build-up each side). BT resin core 800~$\mu$m, plus 100~$\mu$m each side build-up = 1000~$\mu$m total. LGA pad finish: ENIG (Au 0.05~$\mu$m over Ni 3--6~$\mu$m).

\subsection{LGA 4677 substrate}

10-layer build-up substrate: 2-4-2 (one thick core, four layers each side). Total thickness 1400~$\mu$m. Same LGA pad finish.

\section{Heatsink Mounting}

\subsection{FCBGA 16$\times$16 (Ph-1)}

The package has no separate heatsink mounting. The phone OEM attaches a thin vapor chamber or heat spreader directly to the lid via the TIM interface. The mounting is by adhesive or snap-fit, not by screws.

\begin{itemize}
\item TIM contact area: 12.0~mm $\times$ 12.0~mm (the lid top surface, minus a 2~mm border for seal).
\item TIM thickness: 0.10--0.20~mm.
\item Mounting pressure: 5--15 psi (typical for adhesive-based phone cooling).
\end{itemize}

\subsection{LGA 1700 (Pc-1)}

The package uses the standard Intel LGA 1700 heatsink mounting hole pattern (ILM, Independent Loading Mechanism). The board should provide:

\begin{itemize}
\item 4 mounting holes on a rectangular pitch, 75.0~mm $\times$ 82.0~mm ($\pm$0.10~mm).
\item Hole diameter: 3.5~mm ($\pm$0.05~mm).
\item Board mounting pad: 6.0~mm diameter (for M3.5 screws).
\item Mounting pressure: 30--50 lbf (typical for 360~mm AIO), applied via the AIO backplate.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{LGA 4677 (Dc-1S)}

The package uses the standard Intel LGA 4677 heatsink mounting hole pattern (Eagle Stream socket):

\begin{itemize}
\item 4 mounting holes on a rectangular pitch, 100.0~mm $\times$ 115.0~mm ($\pm$0.15~mm).
\item Hole diameter: 4.0~mm ($\pm$0.05~mm).
\item Board mounting pad: 7.0~mm diameter (for M4 screws).
\item Mounting pressure: 50--80 lbf (typical for cold-plate cooling).
\item Torque specification: 8--10 in-lb per screw (M4 stainless steel, 4 screws).
\end{itemize}

\section{Thermal Interface Material (TIM)}

\subsection{TIM specification}

The package lid is supplied with no factory-applied TIM. The user applies TIM between the lid and the heatsink/cold-plate.

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{4cm}p{3cm}p{2cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Property} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Tolerance} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Property} & \textbf{Value} & \textbf{Tolerance} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Thermal conductivity & $\ge$ 8~W/m$\cdot$K & --- & For Pc-1 (high TDP) \\
Thermal conductivity (DC) & $\ge$ 12~W/m$\cdot$K & --- & For Dc-1S \\
Thermal conductivity (Phone) & $\ge$ 3~W/m$\cdot$K & --- & For Ph-1 \\
Thickness (applied) & 0.05--0.20~mm & --- & Based on TIM type \\
Operating temperature & $-$40 to $+$150~°C & --- & --- \\
Interface resistance & $\le$ 0.05~K$\cdot$cm$^2$/W & --- & For 8~W/m$\cdot$K paste \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{TIM application}

For Pc-1 and Dc-1S, apply TIM paste as a 5-dot pattern (center + 4 corners) on the IHS top surface, then mount the cooler. The mounting pressure spreads the TIM to 0.05--0.15~mm uniform thickness. Do not apply more than 0.5~g of TIM; excess can bridge to the substrate edge.

For Ph-1, apply TIM pad (0.15~mm thickness) between the lid and the vapor chamber.

\section{Solder Reflow Profile}

\subsection{Reflow profile for board assembly}

Standard SAC305 lead-free reflow, with the following profile (measured at the ball):

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3.5cm}p{2.5cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Phase} & \textbf{Temperature} & \textbf{Time} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Phase} & \textbf{Temperature} & \textbf{Time} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Preheat & 25 $\to$ 150~°C & 60--90~s (max 3~°C/s ramp) \\
Soak & 150 $\to$ 200~°C & 60--120~s \\
Reflow & Peak 245 $\pm$ 5~°C & 20--40~s above 217~°C (liquidus) \\
Cooling & 245 $\to$ 25~°C & 60--90~s (max 6~°C/s down-ramp) \\
\midrule
\textbf{Total cycle} & --- & 200--330~s \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\subsection{Reflow constraints}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Peak temperature:} 245~°C max. Higher peak increases intermetallic growth and reduces joint reliability.
\item \textbf{Time above liquidus:} 20--40~s. Longer causes excessive IMC formation.
\item \textbf{Ramp rates:} $\le$3~°C/s up, $\le$6~°C/s down. Faster causes thermal shock and potential die cracking.
\item \textbf{Number of reflows:} The chip can survive 3 reflows (for double-sided assembly + rework). Pre-wake-up applied at factory test is designed to withstand 3 reflows.
\item \textbf{MSL:} MSL 3 (168 hours floor life at 30~°C / 60\% RH after bake). Higher MSL requires dry-pack storage.
\end{itemize}

\section{Moisture Sensitivity Level (MSL)}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{2cm}p{3cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Package} & \textbf{MSL} & \textbf{Floor life} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Package} & \textbf{MSL} & \textbf{Floor life} & \textbf{Notes} \\
\midrule
\endhead
FCBGA 16$\times$16 & MSL 3 & 168~h & Standard \\
LGA 1700 & MSL 3 & 168~h & Standard \\
LGA 4677 & MSL 3 & 168~h & Standard \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The packages ship in dry-pack (moisture barrier bag + desiccant + HIC). The bag is opened only when the board is ready to reflow. After opening, the floor life of 168 hours at $\le$30~°C / $\le$60\% RH applies. If exceeded, the package is baked at 125~°C for 24 hours before use.

\section{Mechanical Stress and Reliability}

\subsection{Thermal cycling}

The package must survive $-$40 to $+$125~°C thermal cycling, 1000 cycles, no mechanical or electrical failure.

\subsection{Drop and shock}

For Ph-1 (phone product): must survive 1.5~m drop onto concrete, 26 orientations, no electrical failure. The phone OEM encloses the package in its chassis; the chassis handles the drop energy.

For Pc-1 and Dc-1S: no drop test (server and desktop form factors).

\subsection{Vibration}

All three packages must survive 5--500 Hz random vibration at 1.5~g RMS, 30 min per axis, no failure.

\section{Assembly Drawings Package}

The mechanical drawing package ships separately and contains:

\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{fhpf\_ph1\_package.dxf} — full PCB footprint, top-side component, and keep-out
\item \texttt{fhpf\_pc1\_package.dxf} — same
\item \texttt{fhpf\_dc1s\_package.dxf} — same
\item \texttt{fhpf\_ph1\_package\_stackup.pdf} — substrate stack-up drawing
\item \texttt{fhpf\_pc1\_package\_stackup.pdf} — same
\item \texttt{fhpf\_dc1s\_package\_stackup.pdf} — same
\item \texttt{fhpf\_heatsink\_pc1.dwg} — recommended heatsink reference design
\item \texttt{fhpf\_coldplate\_dc1s.dwg} — recommended cold-plate reference design
\item \texttt{fhpf\_dimensions\_check.pdf} — all dimensions with GD\&T callouts
\end{itemize}

The DXF files are the board designer's authoritative reference for the physical footprint. The PDF stack-up drawings give the exact layer composition, prepreg types, and copper weight. The heatsink/cold-plate DWG files are provided as reference designs; the system integrator may substitute alternative cooling solutions that meet the mechanical and thermal specifications in this appendix.
