%=====================================================================
\part*{Appendix G: Bring-Up Checklist}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendix G: Bring-Up Checklist}
%=====================================================================

This appendix is the acceptance test that runs when the first silicon returns from the foundry. It defines, in order, every measurement that confirms the silicon is functional, with acceptance criteria, failure diagnosis, and recovery action for each. It is the checklist a bring-up engineer follows on the bench.

The checklist is organized in 12 stages. Each stage has a precondition (the prior stage passed), a list of measurements, and an exit criterion. A stage that fails blocks all later stages; the failure diagnosis in each stage describes the most likely causes and the recovery actions.

Every measurement is stated with the instrument (oscilloscope, multimeter, logic analyzer, etc.), the pin or register under test, and the numeric acceptance criterion. Nothing in this appendix is optional: all 12 stages must pass before silicon is deemed functional.

\section{Stage 1: Unpowered Continuity and Isolation}

\textbf{Preconditions:} board assembled, chip not powered, no power supply connected.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{VDD\_CORE short to VSS:} multimeter in resistance mode, measure from any VDD\_CORE ball to any VSS ball. Acceptance: $>10$~$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{VDD\_IO short to VSS:} same. Acceptance: $>10$~$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{VDD\_ANALOG short to VSS:} same. Acceptance: $>10$~$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{VDD\_PLL short to VSS:} same. Acceptance: $>10$~$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{VDD\_CORE short to VDD\_IO:} measure between rails. Acceptance: $>1$~k$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{VDD\_CORE short to VDD\_ANALOG:} same. Acceptance: $>1$~k$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{Signal ball isolation:} select 10 signal balls at random (including at least one diff pair per interface). Measure to VSS. Acceptance: $>1$~M$\Omega$.
\item \textbf{Socket contacts:} for LGA 1700 and LGA 4677, visually inspect socket pins under 20$\times$ magnification; bent pins are rejects.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 8 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Short on a rail: board assembly defect (solder bridge, misplaced component). BGA rework required.
\item Short between rails: package defect or board short. Chip replacement likely required.
\item Short on a signal: board assembly defect or chip ESD damage.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 2: Rail Bring-Up (No Chip Load)}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 1 passed; chip on board but not powered.

\textbf{Procedure:} connect bench supply to VDD\_CORE only, with current limit at 100~mA. No other rails powered. Set voltage to 0.50~V.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Current draw at 0.50~V:} acceptance: $<50$~mA.
\item \textbf{VDD\_CORE voltage:} measured at the chip ball (Kelvin connection). Acceptance: 0.50 $\pm$ 0.02~V.
\item \textbf{Repeat for each rail:} VDD\_IO (1.8~V, acceptance $<100$~mA), VDD\_ANALOG (1.5~V, acceptance $<50$~mA), VDD\_PLL (1.0~V, acceptance $<20$~mA).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} current draws within specification at nominal rail voltages.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Excessive current at 0.5~V: chip defect (latch-up, ESD damage) or board short. Chip replacement.
\item Excessive current at nominal VDD\_ANALOG: analog block defect. Chip replacement.
\item Excessive current at VDD\_PLL: PLL defect. Chip replacement.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 3: Power Sequencing}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 2 passed. All rails connected; bench supply replaced with board VRMs.

\textbf{Procedure:} apply main power input; measure each rail's rise time and sequence order against the specification in Appendix A §A.11.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Rail 1 (VDD\_IO) rise time:} acceptance: 0.1--10~ms, monotonic.
\item \textbf{Rail 2 (VDD\_ANALOG) rise time:} acceptance: 0.1--10~ms, monotonic, after Rail 1 (delay 1--10~ms).
\item \textbf{Rail 3 (VDD\_PLL) rise time:} acceptance: 0.1--5~ms, after Rail 2.
\item \textbf{Rail 4 (VDD\_CORE) rise time:} acceptance: 0.1--50~ms, after Rail 3.
\item \textbf{POR release:} RESET pin rises after all rails are within 5\% of nominal. Acceptance: POR rise time 1--100~ms after last rail OK.
\item \textbf{Rail\_STATUS register:} read via JTAG (no CPU yet). Acceptance: RAIL\_STATUS = 0x3F (all 6 rails).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 6 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Wrong sequencing: PMIC or board sequencing circuit defect. Rework.
\item Rail rise time out of spec: VRM design issue. Rework.
\item Rail\_STATUS not all 6 bits: some rail not OK. Check that rail's VRM.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 4: PLL Lock and Clock Distribution}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 3 passed. Chip is powered, POR released.

\textbf{Procedure:} check the compute PLL and the CXL LC-PLL lock via JTAG status register; measure clock at the package pin.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Compute PLL lock:} read via JTAG (offset 0x4000\_9000 register 0x20, bit 12). Acceptance: LOCK = 1 within 10~ms of POR release.
\item \textbf{Compute PLL frequency:} measure at a clock test point (if the package brings one out; otherwise measure via the CPU clock counter over a known wall-clock interval). Acceptance: 6.20 $\pm$ 0.03~GHz on Pc-1, 4.50 $\pm$ 0.03~GHz on Ph-1, 6.00 $\pm$ 0.03~GHz on Dc-1S.
\item \textbf{CXL LC-PLL lock (Pc-1, Dc-1S):} read via JTAG register 0x4000\_9000 register 0x24, bit 12. Acceptance: LOCK = 1.
\item \textbf{Clock jitter:} measure via spectrum analyzer (if external clock is available) or via the CPU clock noise floor. Acceptance: $<$1.0~ps RMS integrated 100 Hz--100 MHz.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item PLL not locking: PLL reference absent or wrong frequency. Check the reference crystal / oscillator on the board.
\item PLL locking at wrong frequency: divider misconfiguration. Check the boot OTP settings or the strapping pins.
\item Jitter $>1$~ps: PLL design margin; check the reference quality and VDD\_PLL noise.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 5: JTAG and Boot ROM Execution}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 4 passed. PLL locked.

\textbf{Procedure:} connect JTAG; halt the boot hart at the boot ROM entry point; step through the first 100 instructions; confirm the boot ROM signature and the first-branch target.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{JTAG TAP IDCODE:} acceptance: reads the expected device ID (0x1D1F\_0001 for Ph-1, 0x1D1F\_0002 for Pc-1, 0x1D1F\_0003 for Dc-1S).
\item \textbf{Boot ROM entry:} after POR + JTAG halt, PC = 0x0000\_0000\_0000\_0000. Acceptance: exact.
\item \textbf{First instruction:} read memory at 0x0. Acceptance: matches the boot ROM image.
\item \textbf{Boot ROM signature:} read the last 16 bytes of the boot ROM region. Acceptance: matches the expected ECDSA-public-key hash (readable at 0x0000\_0000\_0000\_0800).
\item \textbf{First branch target:} step to the first `j` or `jal` instruction; confirm the target is inside the boot ROM address range (0x0 to 0x1000).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 5 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item JTAG IDCODE wrong: chip defect (mask ROM or TAP issue) or JTAG pin damage. Chip replacement.
\item Boot ROM entry not 0: boot strapping or boot selection strapping pins wrong. Check board straps.
\item Boot ROM signature mismatch: die-level mask issue. Chip replacement.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 6: Boot ROM Completion}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 5 passed. JTAG verified boot ROM start.

\textbf{Procedure:} remove JTAG halt; allow the boot ROM to run to completion; capture the boot ROM UART output.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{UART output at 115200 8N1:} acceptance: firmware banner appears within 250~ms of POR release.
\item \textbf{DDR5 training status:} captured in the UART log. Acceptance: "DDR5 training: PASS."
\item \textbf{PCIe enumeration (Pc-1, Dc-1S):} captured in the UART log. Acceptance: "PCIe: N devices enumerated" with N $\ge$ 1 (assuming a PCIe device is populated).
\item \textbf{CXL enumeration (Pc-1, Dc-1S, multi-socket only):} captured in the UART log. Acceptance: "CXL: M remote sockets" with M $\ge$ 1 (single-socket: M = 0).
\item \textbf{ACPI tables or DT blob loaded:} captured in the UART log. Acceptance: "Platform description: [ACPI | DT] at address 0xXXXXXXXX."
\item \textbf{Jump to OS loader:} captured in the UART log. Acceptance: "Jump to OS loader."
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 6 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item No UART output: UART pin misrouted; UART clock absent; boot ROM hung. Check Stage 5.
\item DDR5 training fail: memory signal integrity; check the DDR5 trace length and impedance (Appendix E §E.10).
\item PCIe enumeration 0: PCIe device not populated or link training fail. Check the PCIe diff pair polarity.
\item ACPI/DT not loaded: boot OTP boot-mode bit wrong.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 7: DDR5 Memory Test}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 6 passed. DDR5 training passed; boot ROM passed to OS loader.

\textbf{Procedure:} halt at the OS loader entry; run a memory test utility (memtest86+, or the design-supplied \texttt{dramtest}) over the full DRAM range.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Memtest86+ full pass:} acceptance: 0 errors over the full memory.
\item \textbf{Bandwidth:} measure with \texttt{stream} or \texttt{mbw}. Acceptance: $\ge$400~GB/s on Pc-1 (8 channels), $\ge$200~GB/s on Dc-1S (4 channels), $\ge$100~GB/s on Ph-1 (2 LPDDR5X channels).
\item \textbf{Latency:} measure with \texttt{lmbench} or \texttt{Intel MLC}. Acceptance: 38--54~ns.
\item \textbf{Refresh and ECC:} enable ECC, inject single-bit errors via register writes; confirm correction. Acceptance: single-bit corrected, double-bit detected.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 4 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Memtest errors: DRAM defective, DRAM timing marginal, DDR5 signal integrity. Check with reduced clock (DDR5-5600 instead of 7200) to isolate.
\item Low bandwidth: one channel not training; check DDR5 channel enable in the boot ROM log.
\item High latency: DDR5 training marginal; retrain at lower frequency.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 8: NVL3 First-Program and Read-Back}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 7 passed. DDR5 functional. NVL3 controller initialized per Appendix B §B.4.

\textbf{Procedure:} run the NVL3 first-program test. This test writes a known pattern (walking-1, walking-0, checkerboard, all-0, all-1) to every bank, reads back, verifies, and checks ECC status.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{NVL3 calibration:} confirm CALIB\_STATUS.DONE = 1 after the boot path's CALIB\_START sequence. Acceptance: done within 20~ms.
\item \textbf{NVL3 first-program test:} acceptance: 0 errors in the pattern write/read-back on all banks.
\item \textbf{NVL3 column leakage:} read the column leak screening per Appendix A §A.16. Acceptance: $>$99\% of columns classified Normal; reduced-margin columns reported.
\item \textbf{NVL3 ECC single-bit:} inject via register; confirm correction. Acceptance: corrected, address logged.
\item \textbf{NVL3 ECC double-bit:} inject via register; confirm detection. Acceptance: detected, address logged, error reported.
\item \textbf{NVL3 retention:} program known pattern, power down for 1 minute (post-factory pre-wake-up should be present), power back up, read back. Acceptance: 0 errors.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 6 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Calibration not done: OTP calibration table corrupt. Rerun factory calibration.
\item First-program errors: HZO film phase fraction low (see Appendix A §A.16). Check Gate 1 acceptance.
\item High column leakage: process marginality. Column screening will flag bad columns.
\item Retention fail without pre-wake-up applied: pre-wake-up missing from this die. Rerun factory test.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 9: Crossbar First-Program and Calibration (Ph-1, Pc-1)}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 8 passed. NVL3 functional. Crossbar controller initialized per Appendix B §B.5.

\textbf{Procedure:} load the calibration table from OTP; program a known weight pattern; run a MAC operation; compare against the expected analog output.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Crossbar calibration:} confirm CALIB\_STATUS.DONE = 1. Acceptance: done within 5~ms.
\item \textbf{DAC monotonicity:} iterate all 16 DAC codes; measure ADC output; acceptance: 16 distinct outputs in increasing order.
\item \textbf{Weight program:} program a row with pattern {00, 01, 10, 11} repeated; read back; acceptance: all cells correct.
\item \textbf{ADC ENOB:} apply a known input ramp; compute ENOB. Acceptance: $\ge$8.5 bits (spec 8.9).
\item \textbf{MAC operation:} program a 16×16 identity matrix; apply a 16-element input vector; compare output to input. Acceptance: output = input within 2 LSB (for each output element).
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 5 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Calibration fail: OTP calibration table corrupt or TIA/ADC marginal. Rerun factory calibration.
\item DAC monotonicity fail: DAC design defect. Chip replacement.
\item Weight program errors: FeFET cell defect or write-verify controller issue. Rerun first-program.
\item ENOB fail: ADC design or VDD\_ANALOG noise. Check the analog rail.
\item MAC mismatch: crossbar analog chain misconfigured. Re-run calibration.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 10: PCIe Enumeration and DMA}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 9 passed (or Stage 8 for Dc-1S). Platform description loaded.

\textbf{Procedure:} after boot into OS loader, run PCIe enumeration. Plug in a known-good NVMe drive; run a PCIe bandwidth test.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{PCIe enumeration:} \texttt{lspci} lists all populated PCIe devices with correct vendor/device IDs. Acceptance: device count matches physical population.
\item \textbf{PCIe link width:} \texttt{lspci -vv} shows the expected width (x16 for the main PCIe slot; x4 for the NVMe slot). Acceptance: matches physical slot.
\item \textbf{PCIe link speed:} shows the expected generation (Gen5 on Pc-1 and Dc-1S; Gen3 on Ph-1). Acceptance: 32 GT/s (Gen5) or 8 GT/s (Gen3).
\item \textbf{PCIe DMA bandwidth:} \texttt{dd} read from the NVMe at sustained queue depth. Acceptance: $\ge$12~GB/s for Gen5 x4, $\ge$3~GB/s for Gen3 x2.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 4 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Device not enumerated: board-level PCIe routing issue (diff pair polarity, length mismatch), or PHY configuration issue. Check diff pair impedance (Appendix E §E.10).
\item Wrong width: one or more lanes not training. Check per-lane status in the CXL control register (Appendix A §A.12).
\item Wrong speed: link training failing at Gen5; retrain at Gen4 or Gen3 to isolate.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 11: Kernel Boot to Userspace}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 10 passed (Dc-1S, Pc-1) or Stage 9 passed (Ph-1). OS image prepared.

\textbf{Procedure:} boot into a full Linux kernel; measure boot time, check dmesg for errors, reach userspace.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Kernel boot time to userspace:} acceptance: $\le$500~ms (spec is 400~ms with 20\% margin).
\item \textbf{dmesg errors:} acceptance: no "ERROR" or "Call trace" lines (warnings may be present).
\item \textbf{CPU enumeration:} \texttt{lscpu} shows the correct number of CPUs at the correct nominal frequency. Acceptance: matches product (10 on Ph-1, 28 on Pc-1, 32 on Dc-1S).
\item \textbf{Memory visible:} \texttt{free -h} shows the correct DRAM size. Acceptance: within 1\% of physical population.
\item \textbf{NVL3 visible:} \texttt{lsblk} or \texttt{/sys/block} shows the NVL3 block device. Acceptance: capacity matches (64 MB on Ph-1/Pc-1, 24 MB on Dc-1S).
\item \textbf{Thermal sensors:} \texttt{sensors} reads all sensors with plausible temperatures (within $\pm$5~°C of ambient + offset). Acceptance: all sensors report values.
\item \textbf{Crossbar visible (Ph-1, Pc-1):} \texttt{/dev/crossbar} device present. Acceptance: device node exists.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 7 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item Boot time >500 ms: DRAM training slow, or ACPI enumeration slow. Check Stage 7 timing.
\item dmesg errors: driver initialization failures. The UART log at the failing driver identifies the block.
\item Wrong CPU count: MADT/DT issue. Check Appendix D.
\item NVL3 invisible: NVL3 driver not loaded. Check the kernel config and the DSDT/DT NVL0 node.
\item Sensors not readable: thermal driver issue. Check Appendix A §A.6 register map.
\end{itemize}

\section{Stage 12: Userspace Verification and Application Run}

\textbf{Preconditions:} Stage 11 passed. Userspace boot complete.

\textbf{Procedure:} run a representative workload to confirm end-to-end functionality.

\textbf{Measurements:}

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{CPU stress test:} \texttt{stress-ng --cpu N --timeout 60s}. Acceptance: no errors, no thermal throttle above threshold.
\item \textbf{Memory stress:} \texttt{stress-ng --vm 4 --vm-bytes 4G --timeout 60s}. Acceptance: no OOM, no errors.
\item \textbf{NVL3 read/write:} \texttt{dd if=/dev/nvl3 of=/dev/null bs=1M} and reverse. Acceptance: read at line-speed, write without verify error.
\item \textbf{Crossbar AI (Ph-1, Pc-1):} run ResNet-50 inference on the crossbar test image. Acceptance: accuracy $\ge$75.5\% (spec 75.7\%, margin 0.2 pp).
\item \textbf{Windows application (Pc-1, Dc-1S):} run Firefox for Windows under Wine+Box64. Acceptance: launches, navigates to a web page, no crash.
\item \textbf{Sleep/wake (P3$\to$P0):} issue sleep, measure wake time. Acceptance: wake time $\le$15~µs.
\item \textbf{Cold boot from NVL3:} power cycle, measure boot time. Acceptance: $\le$500~ms.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Exit criterion:} all 7 measurements pass.

\textbf{Failure diagnosis:}
\begin{itemize}
\item CPU stress failure: CPU microarchitecture bug. Further diagnosis with \texttt{perf}.
\item Crossbar accuracy low: re-run calibration (Stage 9); if still low, crossbar cells defective.
\item Windows app crash: Wine configuration or Box64 issue. Check the compatibility matrix.
\item Sleep/wake slow: NVL3 state not retained, or PLL relock slow. Check the P3 wake path.
\end{itemize}

\section{Bring-Up Summary}

All 12 stages must pass before the silicon is deemed functional. Each stage blocks the next. The total bench time, once a board is assembled and a debug setup is in place, is approximately 4--6 hours for a first-pass run, or 1--2 days if any stage requires debugging. After the first successful bring-up, subsequent boards take 30--60 minutes per board.

The bring-up log (every measurement with the measured value, not just pass/fail) is a required deliverable. It becomes the reference dataset for future silicon revisions.

\section{Bring-Up Equipment List}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{5cm}p{5cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Equipment} & \textbf{Purpose} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Equipment} & \textbf{Purpose} \\
\midrule
\endhead
Bench power supply (0--5~V, 20~A) & Stage 2 \\
Digital multimeter (4.5 digit) & Stages 1, 2, 3 \\
Oscilloscope (500 MHz, 4-channel) & Stages 3, 4 \\
Spectrum analyzer (26.5~GHz) & Stage 4 (jitter) \\
Logic analyzer (16-channel, 500 MS/s) & Stages 5, 6 \\
JTAG debugger (RISC-V compatible) & Stages 4, 5, 6, 8, 9 \\
UART-to-USB adapter (115200 baud) & Stages 6, 11 \\
Thermal camera & Stage 12 \\
PCIe NVMe drive (known-good) & Stage 10 \\
Bootable OS image (with NVL3 driver) & Stage 11 \\
Crossbar test image (ResNet-50) & Stage 12 \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

%=====================================================================
\part*{Appendix H: Security Provisioning Procedure}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Appendix H: Security Provisioning Procedure}
%=====================================================================

This appendix specifies how the security credentials (device-unique ECDSA key, OEM root key hash, debug public key, boot nonce, rollback counter) are generated, injected into OTP, locked, and tracked through the device lifecycle. It is the production procedure for the security provisioning step in factory test, plus the operational procedures for key lifecycle management after the device ships.

The OTP injection sequence itself is in Appendix B §B.7; this appendix provides the surrounding context: where the keys come from, how they are delivered, how the factory systems handle them, and how they are used after provisioning.

\section{Security Lifecycle States}

Every device transitions through four states, tracked by a provisioning status word in OTP region 5:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{2cm}p{3cm}p{4cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{State} & \textbf{Name} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{What is allowed} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{State} & \textbf{Name} & \textbf{Condition} & \textbf{What is allowed} \\
\midrule
\endhead
S0 & Unprovisioned & OTP root key region blank & Factory provisioning only; boot fails secure boot check \\
S1 & Provisioned & Root key, device key, debug key, nonce all programmed; OTP regions 0--4 locked & Normal operation; secure boot enforced \\
S2 & Commissioned & First end-user boot; attestation certificate issued & Normal operation; debug unlock via challenge-response \\
S3 & Decommissioned & Rollback counter set to max; OTP region 0 locked permanently & Boot only with decommission key; no secure boot \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The provisioning status word is at OTP region 5, word 0. It is read at boot; the boot ROM enforces the state transitions. A device that boots with state S0 (unprovisioned) jumps to a recovery image (physical presence required) rather than the normal firmware.

\section{Key Generation}

\subsection{OEM root key}

The OEM root key is an ECDSA P-256 key pair. It is generated by the OEM's security team on an HSM, in the following procedure:

\begin{enumerate}
\item HSM initialization: per the OEM's HSM vendor procedure (e.g., Thales Luna, Entrust nShield, AWS CloudHSM). At least 3 HSM administrators must be present (M-of-N quorum, typical M=2, N=3).
\item Root key generation: \texttt{HSM: generate-ecdsa-p256 --label "FHP-ROOT-2026" --usage sign,verify --exportability none}. The key is generated inside the HSM and never leaves it in plaintext.
\item Root key backup: 5 shares of the root key are stored in geographically separate vaults, encrypted under a per-share key. Recovery requires 3-of-5 shares (Shamir).
\item Root public key extraction: \texttt{HSM: export-public-key --label "FHP-ROOT-2026" --out fhp\_root\_pub.der}. This is the value injected into OTP region 0 at factory test (its hash is what the boot ROM anchors).
\item Root key sign: the OEM uses the root key to sign the OEM bootloader, kernel, initrd, and userspace images. The signing procedure uses the HSM to produce the ECDSA signature; the private key never leaves the HSM.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Device-unique key}

The device-unique ECDSA P-256 key is generated by the factory test system at the time of provisioning:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Factory HSM generates a fresh ECDSA P-256 key for the device: \texttt{HSM: generate-ecdsa-p256 --label "FHP-DEV-<serial>" --usage sign,verify --exportability wrapped}.
\item The private key is exported wrapped under the device's OTP public key (see §H.3), so the wrapper can be opened only by the specific device's security engine.
\item The public key is stored in the factory database (Production Key Management System, PKMS) indexed by device serial.
\item The wrapped private key is delivered to the device via the factory programming interface.
\item The factory HSM zeroizes the plaintext private key after wrapping (the wrapped form remains in PKMS).
\end{enumerate}

The device-unique private key is used for debug unlock challenge-response and attestation signature. It is never exported in plaintext from the device after provisioning.

\subsection{Debug public key}

The debug public key is the OEM's chosen ECDSA P-256 public key for the debugger. It is generated by the OEM and provided to the factory test system as a DER file. The factory injects it into OTP region 2. This key authorizes debug unlock; the OEM can rotate it by generating a new key and issuing a firmware update that authorizes the new key (the old key stays in OTP but firmware rejects unlock requests signed by it).

\subsection{Boot nonce}

The boot nonce is a 16-byte random value generated by the device's TRNG at provisioning time. It is used to derive the per-boot encryption keys (Appendix A §A.12). The TRNG must pass its self-tests (Appendix A §A.13 register TRNG\_STATUS.HEALTH) before the nonce is used. If the TRNG health check fails, the device is flagged for factory RMA.

\section{OTP Injection Sequence}

The detailed OTP write sequence is in Appendix B §B.7. The surrounding factory procedure is:

\begin{enumerate}
\item The device is on the factory ATE at the security provisioning station.
\item The factory system confirms the device is in state S0 (OTP region 0 blank).
\item The factory system is connected via secure channel to the factory HSM and PKMS.
\item The factory system requests a device-unique key pair; the HSM generates it and returns the wrapped private key + public key.
\item The factory system reads the OEM root key hash (from the OEM-provided DER file) and the debug public key (from OEM).
\item The factory system reads the device's TRNG output (16 bytes) via the JTAG/SPI programming interface.
\item The factory system calls the OTP injection sequence (Appendix B §B.7) to write root key hash, device key (wrapped), debug public key, rollback counter = 1, boot nonce.
\item The factory system calls the OTP read-verify for every word written.
\item If all read-verifies pass, the factory system writes OTP region 5 word 0 to the S1 transition value (0x5052\_4F56 = "PROV" in ASCII).
\item The factory system locks OTP regions 0--4 (irreversible).
\item The factory system logs the successful provisioning to PKMS: device serial, public key, timestamp, factory ID, operator ID.
\item The factory system moves the device to the next station.
\end{enumerate}

If any step fails, the device is scrapped and the provisioning log records the failure reason. There is no retry on OTP write failures (OTP is one-time programmable).

\section{Rollback Counter Initialization}

The rollback counter starts at 1 at provisioning time. Each firmware update increments it. The counter is a 32-bit monotonic value stored in OTP region 3. Firmware rejects any update with a version number $\le$ the current counter. The counter is never reset except by decommissioning.

The OTP storage for the counter is 8 bytes (64 bits), with the high 32 bits reserved. The counter increments in place: OTP bits are one-way programmable, so a counter increment writes additional bits (the counter effectively becomes a "one-time programmable multi-bit field" that stores the largest value written, since the read returns the bitwise-OR of all written values in an increment-only encoding). The encoding is binary: counter=1 writes bit 0; counter=2 writes bit 1; counter=3 writes bits 0 and 1 (already 1); etc. Increments work by writing the new value; the read returns the OR of all writes, which equals the highest value written (because the OR of a binary sequence 1, 2, 3, 4... has all bits set that any value set, and the highest value always has a superset of bits of any lower value). The OTP controller must implement this OR-on-write semantics for the rollback counter region specifically (the rest of OTP is write-once).

\section{Debug Credential Delivery}

After provisioning, the device is in state S1 (provisioned). The debug public key is in OTP, but the debugger also needs:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Device serial number:} printed on the package top (2D barcode + human-readable). The factory PKMS holds the mapping serial $\to$ device public key.
\item \textbf{Device public key:} exported from the package via the SPI/I2C debug interface at provisioning time; also available from PKMS by serial.
\item \textbf{Provisioning certificate:} the OEM's root key signs the device public key, producing an X.509-like certificate that the debugger verifies.
\end{enumerate}

The debug certificate is written to an EEPROM on the board (separate from the chip, on the factory-programmed EEPROM) at board assembly time, or sent to the OEM by PKMS (whichever flow the OEM prefers). The debugger reads the certificate from the EEPROM (or from the OEM's PKMS) and uses the certified device public key to verify the challenge-response.

\section{Attestation Key Derivation}

The device-unique ECDSA key is the root of the attestation chain, but it is not used directly for every attestation. Instead, the boot ROM derives a per-boot attestation key from the device key + boot nonce:

\begin{enumerate}
\item Read device private key from OTP (wrapped).
\item Unwrap in the security engine (never exits the security engine).
\item Read boot nonce from OTP.
\item Derive: \texttt{attestation\_key = HKDF-SHA-256(device\_key, boot\_nonce || 0x01, "FHP-AK", 32)}.
\item The attestation key is the ECDSA private key used to sign the DICE attestation quote.
\item The attestation public key is exported via the DICE CDI register (Appendix A §A.13); the OEM's root key signs the attestation public key into an attestation certificate.
\end{enumerate}

The attestation certificate has a validity of 24 hours after boot. A verifier (e.g., a cloud service) checks: (a) the attestation certificate is signed by the OEM root key; (b) the attestation certificate's public key matches the signed quote; (c) the quote's boot measurement matches the expected boot image hash.

\section{Debug Unlock Procedure (Operational)}

The debug unlock procedure at any time after provisioning:

\begin{enumerate}
\item The debugger reads the device serial (visible on the package, or via SPI at bring-up).
\item The debugger retrieves the device public key from the OEM PKMS or from the board EEPROM.
\item The debugger generates a random 32-byte challenge.
\item The debugger writes the challenge to the OTP controller's CHALLENGE register (Appendix A §A.14).
\item The device security engine signs the challenge with the device private key and writes the response to RESPONSE register.
\item The debugger reads the response and verifies it against the device public key.
\item If verification succeeds, the debugger writes VERIFY=1 then UNLOCK=1 to CTRL.
\item The device opens the JTAG port for the configured TIMEOUT (default 1 hour).
\item All subsequent JTAG operations are logged to the device's event log (OTP region 6, 4 KB ring buffer).
\end{enumerate}

Every failed verification attempt increments ATTEMPTS. After 5 failed attempts, LOCK\_DOWN is set; the device permanently refuses JTAG unlock until a factory RMA reverses the counter (the RMA process involves an OEM-signed recovery image that resets the attempts counter once).

\section{Decommissioning}

When the device reaches end of life, or when the OEM determines the device must be permanently disabled (e.g., compromised credentials), the decommission sequence runs:

\begin{enumerate}
\item The OEM issues a "decommission" signed image that the device accepts only when it is signed by the OEM root key and has the special decommission flag.
\item The device, on running the decommission image, writes the OTP rollback counter to max (0xFFFF\_FFFF) and sets OTP region 5 word 0 to the S3 transition value (0x4445\_434F = "DECO").
\item OTP region 0 (root key hash) is left readable but no valid firmware image exists for it (the OEM root key is retired).
\item The device boots only into a stub recovery image that prints "DECOMMISSIONED" to UART and halts.
\item The OEM's PKMS marks the device as decommissioned; the device public key is revoked in the OEM's CRL.
\end{enumerate}

The decommission is irreversible (OTP bits cannot be cleared). The device is functionally dead after decommission; it must be physically destroyed for end-of-life disposal per the OEM's e-waste procedure.

\section{Security Event Logging}

The device maintains an event log in OTP region 6 (4 KB ring buffer). Each entry is 32 bytes: timestamp (8 bytes), event code (1 byte), event-specific payload (23 bytes). Event codes:

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{1cm}p{3.5cm}p{7cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Code} & \textbf{Event} & \textbf{Payload} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Code} & \textbf{Event} & \textbf{Payload} \\
\midrule
\endhead
0x01 & Boot successful & Boot image hash (truncated 8 bytes) \\
0x02 & Boot signature failure & Expected hash, actual hash (truncated) \\
0x03 & Rollback attempt blocked & Attempted version, current version \\
0x04 & Debug unlock requested & Challenge (truncated 8 bytes) \\
0x05 & Debug unlock succeeded & Session duration (seconds) \\
0x06 & Debug unlock failed & Attempt number \\
0x07 & Debug lock-down & --- \\
0x08 & Tamper detected & Sensor (voltage/temp/clock) \\
0x09 & Secure boot mode change & Old mode, new mode \\
0x0A & Firmware update & Old version, new version \\
0x0B & Decommission started & --- \\
0x0C & Factory provisioning & Factory ID, operator ID \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

The event log is write-once (OTP) and is read out at RMA for incident investigation. It cannot be cleared or modified. A device whose event log contains unexpected events (e.g., repeated failed unlocks) is flagged for RMA review even if functionally working.

\section{Summary Table}

\begin{center}
\begin{longtable}{p{3cm}p{2cm}p{3cm}p{4cm}}
\toprule
\textbf{Item} & \textbf{OTP Region} & \textbf{Written When} & \textbf{Locked} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Item} & \textbf{OTP Region} & \textbf{Written When} & \textbf{Locked} \\
\midrule
\endhead
OEM root key hash & 0 & Factory provisioning & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Device public key hash & 1 & Factory provisioning & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Device private key (wrapped) & 1 & Factory provisioning & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Debug public key & 2 & Factory provisioning & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Rollback counter & 3 & On firmware update & Never (write-once) \\
Boot nonce & 4 & Factory provisioning & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Provisioning status & 5 & Factory provisioning & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Event log & 6 & On security events & Never (write-once ring) \\
Calibration tables & 7 & Factory test & Yes (at provisioning) \\
Reserved & 8+ & --- & --- \\
\bottomrule
\end{longtable}
\end{center}

\section{Production Sign-Off}

A device ships from the factory only when the following are all true:

\begin{enumerate}
\item All 12 bring-up stages (Appendix G) passed.
\item OTP provisioning status is S1 (provisioned).
\item All OTP regions 0--5 are locked.
\item The event log contains only the provisioning event (no errors).
\item The device's public key is in the OEM PKMS.
\item The provisioning certificate has been issued (or the OEM has confirmed the public key is in the database).
\item The device passed the final electrical test.
\end{enumerate}

A device that fails any of these is either reworked (if rework is possible) or scrapped.
